) and has an analogous dual interpretation. Namely,
{\cal C}_{\mathsf{A}}^{(\mathsf{E},\mathbbm{1})}(\Sigma)
realizes the one-gauging along
\Sigma
of the finite subgroup
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{E}}\subset[{\rm U}(1)^{(1)}_{\rm e}]^{n}
of the electric one-form symmetry group of the GZ model. Note for instance that, dually to what seen above for
{\cal C}_{\mathsf{A}}^{(\mathbbm{1},\mathsf{M})}
, the insertion of (
4.18
) is non-vanishing only if
(\mathsf{E}^{-1})^{J}{}_{I}\oint G_{J}|_{\Sigma}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. This may be seen from (
4.10
), by imposing the vanishing of the contribution localized on
\Sigma
to the saddle point equations for the bulk gauge fields.

Let us finally consider the case of more general
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{M}
matrices. The first factor appearing in (
4.10
) can still be identified with (
4.18
), as in the
\mathsf{M}=\mathbbm{1}
case.
On the other hand, the second factor produces a modified version of (
4.17
):

\widetilde{\cal C}^{(\mathbbm{1},\mathsf{M})}_{\mathsf{E}}(\Sigma)\equiv\frac{1}{|H^{0}(\Sigma,\Gamma_{\mathsf{M}}^{*})|}\sum_{{\bm{\eta}}\in H^{1}(\Sigma,\Gamma_{\mathsf{M}}^{*})}\exp\left[{\rm i}(\mathsf{M}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}\oint_{\Sigma}\eta_{I}\cup(\mathsf{E}^{J}{}_{K}F^{K})\right]\,.

(4.19)

This again corresponds to a one-gauging of the finite one-form symmetry group
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{M}}
, but now associated with a non-minimal choice of ‘charges’
\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}
.

In conclusion, the general condensation defect (
4.9
) can be written as

\displaystyle{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{M})}_{\mathsf{A}}={\cal C}^{(\mathsf{E},\mathbbm{1})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times\widetilde{\cal C}^{(\mathbbm{1},\mathsf{M})}_{\mathsf{E}}\,.

(4.20)

Finally, we observe that, if
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{M}
are left-coprime and can be simultaneously put in Smith normal form, (
4.19
) is in fact equal to (
4.17
),
so that
{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{M})}_{\mathsf{A}}={\cal C}^{(\mathsf{E},\mathbbm{1})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal C}^{(\mathbbm{1},\mathsf{M})}_{\mathsf{A}}
. This is in agreement with the analogous result of
[
117
]
for the
n=1
case.

4.3
C
interfaces from TQFTs

Consider now the
C
interfaces
{\cal W}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
. As in (
3.7
),
{\cal W}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
should interpolate between

{\cal L}_{\mathsf{C}}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi_{\text{\tiny L}})\equiv{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi_{\text{\tiny L}})+\frac{1}{4\pi}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}\,,

(4.21)

and
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\phi_{\text{\tiny R}})
. The corresponding gluing conditions in (
3.9
) require that the fields
F^{I},\phi^{i}
are continuous across
\Sigma
, while

G_{\text{\tiny L}\,I}|_{\Sigma}=G_{\text{\tiny R}\,I}|_{\Sigma}+\mathsf{C}_{IJ}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}\,,

(4.22)

in agreement with (
4.21
).

A first naive candidate for
{\cal W}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
could be provided by (
4.1b
) with
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Q}
, as one can easily realize by integrating out the world-volume
b_{I}
and computing the boundary contributions to the equations of motion of the bulk gauge fields. However, this possibility would not be invariant under large gauge transformations. As in
[
66
,
23
]
, the way out is to replace (
4.1b
) with an interface

{\cal W}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)={\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}\left[F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]\int{\cal D}b\,\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge(F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}-F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}})\right)\,,

(4.23)

where
{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}\left[F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]
encodes the coupling of a TQFT supported on
\Sigma
to the bulk field strengths
F_{\text{\tiny R}}
, assuming for simplicity that the manifolds are spin. This coupling should be responsible for the generation of the gluing conditions (
4.22
). This happens precisely if
{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}\left[F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]
is such that

\delta\log{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}\left[F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]=-\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}\delta A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\,,

(4.24)

under a general small variation
\delta A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}
. Indeed, taking (
4.24
) into account, the
boundary contributions to the equations of motion of
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}

are
G_{\text{\tiny L}\,I}|_{\Sigma}+{\rm d}b_{I}=0
and
G_{\text{\tiny R}\,I}|_{\Sigma}+\mathsf{C}_{IJ}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}+{\rm d}b_{I}=0
, which imply (
4.22
).

In order to realize (
4.24
), let us first introduce an integral matrix factorization

\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{P}\mathsf{N}^{-1}\,,

(4.25)

where
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\in\mathbb{Z}

[
119
]
, with
\det\mathsf{N}\neq 0
.
As in the discussion of the
A
interfaces, one has a large freedom in the choice of the factorization (
4.25
), which can be substantially removed by requiring that (
4.25
) defines a
right-coprime
factorization – see Appendix
C
. This condition reduces the freedom in the choice of the right-coprime
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{N}
to

\mathsf{P}\rightarrow\mathsf{P}\mathsf{T}\quad,\quad\mathsf{N}\rightarrow\mathsf{N}\mathsf{T}\,,

(4.26)

with
\mathsf{T}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
, while a non-right-coprime factorization is related to a right-coprime one by (
4.26
) with
\mathsf{T}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Z})
and
|\det\mathsf{T}|\geq 2
.

One can then rewrite (
4.24
) as

\displaystyle\delta\log{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}\left[F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{N}^{-1}\delta A_{\text{\tiny R}})^{I}\wedge(\mathsf{N}^{-1}F_{\text{\tiny R}})^{J}\,.

(4.27)

Since
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
is symmetric,
(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\equiv\mathsf{N}^{K}{}_{I}\mathsf{P}_{KJ}=(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{C}\mathsf{N})_{IJ}
is symmetric too. Employing the notation introduced after (
4.11
), we can identify
\frac{1}{2\pi}F_{\text{\tiny R}}^{I}
appearing in (
4.27
) with the integral uplift of an element of
H^{2}(X,\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}})
.
This suggests the following physical realization of (
4.27
).

Consider on
\Sigma
a TQFT with
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
one-form symmetry generated by line operators
L_{{\bf n}}(\gamma)
, where
\gamma\subset\Sigma
is a closed curve and
{\bf n}=\{n^{I}\}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
{\bf n}\simeq{\bf n}+\mathsf{N}{\bf k}
for any
{\bf k}=\{k^{I}\}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}
, represents an element of
\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}}\equiv V_{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathsf{N}V_{\mathbb{Z}}
. These lines must realize the conditions
L_{{\bf n}_{1}}(\gamma)L_{{\bf n}_{2}}(\gamma)=L_{{\bf n}_{1}+{\bf n}_{2}}(\gamma)
and
L_{\mathsf{N}{\bf k}}(\gamma)=\mathds{1}
.
We assume that the rational numbers
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Q}
mod
\mathbb{Z}
determine the mutual braiding of these lines as follows

L_{{\bf n}}(\gamma)L_{{\bf n}^{\prime}}(\gamma^{\prime})=\exp\left(-2\pi{\rm i}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}n^{I}n^{\prime J}\right)L_{{\bf n}^{\prime}}(\gamma^{\prime})\,,

(4.28)

where
\gamma
loops
\gamma^{\prime}
.
This brainding respects the periodicities
{\bf n}\simeq{\bf n}+\mathsf{N}{\bf k}
because of (
4.25
), and is consistent with the exchange of the two lines since
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
is symmetric.
11
11

11

The braiding rule (
4.28
) also determines the spin of the line operators:
h[L_{{\bf n}}]=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}n^{I}n^{J}
mod 1.

More generically, the charged objects are line operators
W(\gamma)
such that

L_{{\bf n}}(\gamma)W(\gamma^{\prime})=\exp\left[2\pi{\rm i}(\mathsf{N}^{-1})^{J}{}_{I}n^{I}q_{J}(W)\right]W(\gamma^{\prime})\,,

(4.29)

where the integers
q_{I}(W)\in\mathbb{Z}
represent the charges of the line. Note that we must make the identification
q_{I}(W)\simeq q_{I}(W)+\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{I}k_{J}
, with
k_{J}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and hence the charges of
W(\gamma)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the elements of
\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{N}}\equiv V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Comparing (
4.29
) and (
4.28
), we see that the topological lines
L_{\bf n}
are charged under their own one-form symmetry, with charges

q_{I}(L_{\bf n})=-n^{J}\mathsf{P}_{JI}\quad~~~\text{mod}\quad\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}\,.

(4.30)

Therefore the charges of the topological lines are determined by the integers
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}
appearing in the factorization (
4.25
), which hence also determine the anomaly of the
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
one-form symmetry of the three-dimensional TQFT. More precisely,
these data are determined by the equivalence class of
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}
, or equivalently
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
, modulo the identifications

\mathsf{P}_{IJ}\simeq\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{IJ}=\mathsf{P}_{IJ}+\mathsf{Z}_{IK}\mathsf{N}^{K}{}_{J}\quad,\quad\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\simeq\mathsf{C}^{\prime}_{IJ}=\mathsf{C}_{IJ}+\mathsf{Z}_{IJ}\,,

(4.31)

for any
\mathsf{Z}_{IK}=\mathsf{Z}_{KI}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The anomaly can be detected by coupling the TQFT to classical
\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}}
background connections
\bm{\omega}\in H^{2}(X,\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}})
. Their integral uplift
\omega^{I}
can be identified with
\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}{\cal B}^{J}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
, where
{\cal B}^{I}
are flat background two-form gauge fields, defined modulo
2\pi H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.
Following
[
120
]
,
12
12

12

Our discussion can be connected to Appendix F of
[
120
]
by choosing
\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}=\delta^{I}_{J}N_{J}
. With this specific choice,
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}=\mathsf{P}_{IJ}/N_{J}
, which can be translated to the notation of
[
120
]
by identifying our
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}
and
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
with
m_{JI}
and
P_{IJ}/N_{IJ}
therein. One can always make such a choice by combining a transformation (
4.26
) with
\mathsf{T}\in
GL
(n,\mathbb{Z})
and a GL
(n,\mathbb{Z})
redefinition of the field strengths
F^{I}
.

the anomaly
is then determined by the four-dimensional term

-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{X}{\cal B}^{I}\wedge{\cal B}^{J}\,,

(4.32)

where
Y
is an auxiliary four-dimensional space such that
\partial Y=\Sigma
, and we are assuming that the theory is spin. Under an infinitesimal gauge transformation
\delta{\cal B}^{I}={\rm d}\delta\Lambda^{I}
, the TQFT partition function then transforms by the phase

\exp\left(-\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{\Sigma}\delta\Lambda^{I}\wedge{\cal B}^{I}\right)\,.

(4.33)

Comparing (
4.33
) and (
4.24
), it is clear that a good candidate for
{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny L}}]
appearing in (
4.23
) is provided by the partition function of a TQFT with anomaly determined by the integers
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}
and coupled to bulk two-form gauge fields

{\cal B}^{I}=(\mathsf{N}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\,.

(4.34)

Notice that, since the anomaly is determined by
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}
only modulo (
4.31
), the partition function of a given TQFT with such an anomaly may satisfy (
4.24
) only up to an additional term of the form
\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\mathsf{Z}_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}\delta A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)
, for some
\mathsf{Z}_{IJ}=\mathsf{Z}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
However such a term can always be reabsorbed by a counterterm of the form

\exp\left(-\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\mathsf{Z}_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\,,

(4.35)

corresponding to an additional integral shift of the constants
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
. In the following, we will always assume that this possible counterterm has been included in
{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]
, so that the the resulting interface
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C}}
induces the correct gluing condition (
4.22
) for any
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Q}
.

As a simple example, we can use the
{\cal Z}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}
TQFT defined by the action

-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}a^{I}\wedge f^{J}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\oint_{\Sigma}c_{I}\wedge f^{J}\,,

(4.36)

where
a_{I}
and
c_{I}
are U(1) one-form potentials, and
f^{I}={\rm d}a^{I}
. One can check that this theory is invariant under (
4.31
) using the redefinition
c_{I}\rightarrow c_{I}+\mathsf{Z}_{IJ}\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{K}a^{K}
.
The topological lines generating the
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
one-form symmetry are explicitly given by

L_{\bf n}(\gamma)=\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}n^{I}\oint_{\gamma}c_{I}\right)\,,

(4.37)

and the bulk two-form gauge fields
{\cal B}^{I}
can be coupled by adding a term
\frac{1}{2\pi}N^{I}{}_{J}\oint_{\Sigma}c_{I}\wedge{\cal B}^{J}
to (
4.36
).
According to our general prescription (
4.34
), we then get the following choice of the first factor on the r.h.s. of (
4.23
):

\displaystyle{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]

\displaystyle\equiv{\cal Z}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}[\mathsf{N}^{-1}F_{\text{\tiny R}}]

(4.38)

\displaystyle=\int{\cal D}a{\cal D}c\exp\Big[-\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}a^{I}\wedge f^{J}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}c_{I}\wedge(\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}f^{J}+F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}})\Big]\,.

We can now more explicitly check our general arguments. First, the
a^{I}
and
c_{I}
equations of motion impose

\mathsf{P}_{IJ}f^{J}={\rm d}c_{I}\quad,\quad\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}f^{J}=-F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\rm d}c_{I}=-C_{IJ}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}\,.

(4.39)

On the other hand, the boundary contribution to the bulk
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L},\text{\tiny R}}
equations of motion is

G_{\text{\tiny L}\,I}|_{\Sigma}+{\rm d}b_{I}=0\quad,\quad G_{\text{\tiny R}\,I}|_{\Sigma}-{\rm d}c_{I}+{\rm d}b_{I}=0\,.

(4.40)

Combining them with (
4.39
) and (
4.25
), we get the correct gluing conditions (
4.22
), without any need to include a counterterm of the form (
4.35
).

Notice that
\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P}\rightarrow\mathsf{T}^{\rm t}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})\mathsf{T}
under (
4.26
). If
\mathsf{T}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
, such a transformation can be reabsorbed by a redefinition
a^{I}\rightarrow(\mathsf{T}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}a^{J}
.
This means that all right-coprime factorizations (
4.25
) give the same (
4.38
), which is then unique and depends only on
\mathsf{C}
. We may then write
{\cal I}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}={\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}
.
13
13

13

As a further simple check, suppose that
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Z}
. In this case, the easiest right-coprime factorization (
4.25
) is obtained by picking
\mathsf{N}=\mathds{1}
and
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{C}
. One can then explicitly integrate out
c_{I}
from (
4.38
), which amounts to just setting
a^{I}=A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
. Using the resulting (
4.38
) in (
4.23
), the latter reduces to (
4.1b
).
For non-right-coprime factorizations, (
4.38
) and the corresponding interface (
4.23
) are instead in one-to-one correspondence with the
\mathsf{T}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
conjugacy classes of the integral matrices
\mathsf{T}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
with
|\det\mathsf{T}|\geq 2
.

4.3.1
Minimal
C
interfaces

The choice (
4.38
) is concrete and simple, but any other TQFT with anomaly determined by (
4.32
) could be used to get the appropriate gluing conditions. It would be natural to require some notion of ‘minimality’ on the choice of such TQFT. Following
[
120
]
, a minimal TQFT can identified if and only of the braiding (
4.28
) is non-degenerate, namely, if and only if the only line that braids trivially with all the other lines is the trivial identity line. One can more precisely formulate this condition as follows
[
120
]
. Consider the relation (
4.30
) as defining a linear map
M:\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}}\rightarrow\text{Hom}(\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}},\text{U}(1))
. The braiding (
4.28
) is non-degenerate if and only if
\ker M
is trivial. In such a case the symmetry lines
L_{\bf n}(\gamma)
form a modular three-dimensional TQFT, which one can identify with the
minimal
TQFT with
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
one-form symmetry. As we are now going to show,
\ker M
is trivial, and therefore there exists a minimal TQFT, precisely when the factorization (
4.25
) is right-coprime! Hence, given any
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
, by picking a right-coprime factorization (
4.25
), one can costruct a corrisponding minimal TQFT formed just by the
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
topological lines
L_{\bf n}
with brading (
4.28
).

The proof goes as follows. First notice that the triviality of
\ker M
is equivalent to requiring that any integral vector
{\bf n}=\{n^{I}\}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is such that
\mathsf{C}{\bf n}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}
only if
{\bf n}\in\mathsf{N}V_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
But from (
4.25
), this is equivalent to require that
\mathsf{P}{\bm{\alpha}}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
{\bm{\alpha}}\equiv\mathsf{N}^{-1}{\bf n}
for some
{\bf n}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}
, necessarily implies that
{\bm{\alpha}}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Hence
\ker M
is trivial if and only if any
{\bm{\alpha}}\in V_{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
\mathsf{P}{\bm{\alpha}}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathsf{N}{\bm{\alpha}}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}
is necessarily integral,
{\bm{\alpha}}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}
. That is,
\ker M
is trivial if and only if
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{N}
define a right-coprime factorization – see Appendix
C
– as we wanted to show.

For any given
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
, we can pick a right-coprime factorization (
4.25
) and construct the corresponding minimal TQFT, which actually depends only on the equivalence class under (
4.31
).
14
14

14

Note that
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{N})
define a right-coprime factorization of
\mathsf{C}
if and only if
(\mathsf{P}^{\prime},\mathsf{N})
define a right-coprime factorization of
\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
, with
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
defined as in (
4.31
).
This minimal TQFT is just formed by the
\Gamma^{(1)}_{N}
topological lines
L_{\bf n}
with braiding (
4.28
). Notice that a general change of right-coprime factorization, which is given by (
4.26
) with
\mathsf{T}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
, corresponds just to a relabeling of the lines
L_{\bf n}
. Hence, the minimal TQFT is in fact unique and depends only on
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
mod
\mathbb{Z}
. For clarity, we will nevertheless denote it as
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}
.

As a simple example of minimal TQFT, suppose that (
4.25
) can be realized with
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}=\delta_{IJ}
, i.e. that there exists an integral matrix
\mathsf{N}^{IJ}\equiv(\mathsf{N}\mathsf{P}^{-1})^{IJ}=\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{K}\delta^{KJ}
which is just the inverse of
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
. It is easy to check that this is a trivial example of right-coprime factorization. Then the minimal TQFT can be relized as the following abelian Chern-Simons theory

\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\mathsf{N}^{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}a_{I}\wedge f_{J}\,.

(4.41)

The topological lines generating
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
are

L_{\bf n}(\gamma)=\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}n^{I}\oint_{\gamma}a_{I}\right)\,.

(4.42)

The bulk two-form gauge fields
{\cal B}^{I}
can be coupled by adding
\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\oint_{\Sigma}a_{I}\wedge{\cal B}^{J}
to (
4.41
). Hence, applying the prescription (
4.34
), the corresponding
\mathsf{C}
interface is given by (
4.23
) with

\displaystyle{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny L}}]

\displaystyle={\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathbbm{1})}_{\Sigma}[\mathsf{N}^{-1}F_{\text{\tiny R}}]

(4.43)

\displaystyle=\int{\cal D}a\exp\Big[\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\mathsf{N}^{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}a_{I}\wedge f_{J}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}a_{I}\wedge F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}\Big]\,.

One can easily verify that, with such a choice, (
4.23
) provides the correct gluing conditions.

More generically, one can realize the minimal TQFT on the boundary of a four-dimensional theory, along the lines of
[
121
,
1
,
120
]
. This construction is discussed in detail in Appendix
E
. By applying the general prescription (
4.34
), we then get the following minimal realization of the
\mathsf{C}
defect (
4.23
). Pick an auxiliary four-dimensional space
Y
such that
\partial Y=\Sigma
and a right-coprime integral factorization (
4.25
). Then identify
{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny L}}]
in (
4.23
) with the partition function
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}[\mathsf{N}^{-1}F_{\text{\tiny R}}]
of the topological theory (
E.14
) with
{\cal B}^{I}=(\mathsf{N}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}
:

-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}B^{I}\wedge B^{J}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\int_{Y}B^{J}\wedge\tilde{F}_{I}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}\tilde{A}_{I}\wedge F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}\,,

(4.44)

where
\tilde{A}_{I}
and
B^{I}
are dynamical one- and two-form potentials defined on
Y
. The integration over
\tilde{A}_{I}
imposes that
B^{I}
are flat and identify
\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}}
Wilson lines, and also enforces the modified Dirchlet boundary conditions for
B^{I}
:

\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}B^{J}|_{\Sigma}=-F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}\,.

(4.45)

Together with the
B^{I}
equations of motion
(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}B^{J}=\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{I}\tilde{F}_{J}
, we have that

\quad\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{I}\tilde{F}_{J}|_{\Sigma}=-\mathsf{P}_{JI}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}\,.

(4.46)

which follows from
\mathsf{N}^{t}\mathsf{P}\mathsf{N}^{-1}=\mathsf{P}^{t}
and the fact that
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}=\mathsf{C}_{JI}
.
Moreover, the last term in
(
4.44
), together with the last factor appearing in (
4.23
), affects the boundary
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}
equations of motion as follows:

G_{{\text{\tiny R}}\,I}|_{\Sigma}-\tilde{F}_{I}|_{\Sigma}+{\rm d}b_{I}=0\,.

(4.47)

Combining them with (
4.46
) and the boundary
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
equations of motion
G_{{\text{\tiny L}}\,I}|_{\Sigma}+{\rm d}b_{I}=0
, we again get the correct gluing conditions (
4.22
), without having to include a counterterm of the form (
4.35
).
15
15

15

For
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Z}
, one can again use the right-coprime factorization
\mathsf{N}=\mathds{1}
and
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{C}
and check that, using the TQFT defined by (
4.44
), (
4.23
) reduces to (
4.1b
).

Following
[
120
]
, we can also show that any other three-dimensional TQFT
\tilde{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C}}
with
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
one-form symmetry satisfying (
4.30
), can be factorized into

\tilde{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}={\cal A}^{0}_{\Sigma}\otimes{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}\,,

(4.48)

where
{\cal A}^{0}_{\Sigma}
is a TQFT invariant under the
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
one-form symmetry.
By assumption
\tilde{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}
includes the topological lines
L_{\bf n}
, which by themselves form the minimal TQFT
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}
. Consider next any other line
W
of
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}
, with charges given by
q_{I}(W)
mod
\mathsf{N^{\rm t}}V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. By fusing
W
and with any
L_{\bf n}
, one gets another line
W^{\prime}=WL_{\bf n}
of charges
q_{I}(W^{\prime})=q_{I}(W)-n^{J}\mathsf{P}_{JI}
mod
\mathsf{N^{\rm t}}V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. We would like to show that one can always pick
n^{I}
so that the

\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
charge of
W^{\prime}
is vanishing, namely such that

q_{I}(W)-n^{J}\mathsf{P}_{JI}=k_{J}\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{I}\,,

(4.49)

for some
k_{J}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Again, this follows from the fact that
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{N}
are right-coprime. Indeed, by definition – see Appendix
C
– there exist two integral matrices
\mathsf{X}^{IJ}
and
\mathsf{Y}^{I}{}_{J}
such that

(\mathsf{X}\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{Y}\mathsf{N})^{I}{}_{J}=\delta^{I}{}_{J}\,.

(4.50)

By using this identity it is easy to see that
n^{I}=q_{J}(W)X^{JI}
satisfies (
4.49
), with
k_{I}=-q_{J}(W)\mathsf{Y}^{J}{}_{I}
.
Hence, with such a choice, the line
W^{\prime}=WL_{\bf n}
is
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
neutral.
We can then identify the set of such neutral lines with
{\cal A}^{0}_{\Sigma}
in (
4.48
), since it is clear that any line
W
is the product of a line of
{\cal A}^{0}_{\Sigma}
and a line of
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}
.

Consider, for instance, the
{\cal Z}^{(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{N})}_{\Sigma}
theory (
4.36
). In addition to the
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
lines (
4.37
), it contains also the topological lines

W_{\bf n}(\gamma)=\exp\left[{\rm i}n^{I}\oint_{\gamma}\left(\mathsf{P}_{IJ}a^{J}-c_{I}\right)\right]\,.

(4.51)

One can check that the
L_{\bf n}
and
W_{\bf m}
lines are mutually transparent. Hence the
W_{\bf m}
lines form the decoupled sector
{\cal A}^{0}_{\Sigma}
. Note that
W_{\bf m}
have braiding of the form (
4.28
) with
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\rightarrow-\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
. Hence, we can identify
{\cal A}^{0}_{\Sigma}
with
{\cal A}^{(-\mathsf{P},\mathsf{N})}_{\Sigma}
.

In conclusion, for any
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Q}
we can construct a minimal interface
{\cal W}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
, by choosing

{\cal I}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]={\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}[\mathsf{N}^{-1}F_{\text{\tiny R}}]\,,

(4.52)

in (
4.23
), i.e. the partition function of the minimal TQFT coupled to bulk two-form gauge potentials
{\cal B}^{I}
of the form (
4.34
), plus a possible counterterm (
4.35
). Furthermore, any other
\widetilde{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
constructed by using any other TQFT associated with a right-coprime factorization, can be written as

\widetilde{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)={\cal T}_{0}(\Sigma)\,{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)\,,

(4.53)

where
{\cal T}_{0}(\Sigma)
is the partition function of the neutral sector
{\cal A}^{0}_{\Sigma}
appearing in (
4.48
).
More generically, one can also construct
\mathsf{C}
interfaces by using TQFT with
\Gamma^{(1)}_{N}
one-form symmetry associated with non-necessarily right-coprime factorizations, as for instance the TQFT defined by (
4.38
), which works for any factorization.

As in the case of
\mathsf{A}
defect, in concrete examples, the minimal interfaces may often not be the simplest ones. In these cases, one may use non-minimal ones, possibly associated with non-coprime factorizations of
\mathsf{C}
. In particular, (
4.38
) provides a simple concrete choice, which works for any factorization.

4.3.2
Condensation defects

Similarly to what already seen for the
\mathsf{A}
defects, the non-trivial TQFT supported by the
\mathsf{C}
interface implies that, if
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the fusion
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C}}\times\overline{{\cal W}}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
does not give the identity, but rather a condensation defect
{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
of the type discussed in
[
118
]
.

In order to illustrate this point, let us restrict ourselves to the case
\mathsf{P}=\mathbbm{1}
, that is,
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{N}^{-1}
. In this case the minimal TQFT contribution to (
4.23
) is given by (
4.43
), with
\mathsf{N}^{IJ}\equiv\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{K}\delta^{KJ}
. By fusing
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathbbm{1})}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\overline{{\cal W}}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathbbm{1})}_{\mathsf{C}}
we then get the condensation defect

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathbbm{1})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)=

\displaystyle\int Da\,D\tilde{a}\,Db\,D\tilde{b}\,Dc\;{\rm exp}\bigg[\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\mathsf{N}^{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}\left(a_{I}\wedge{\rm d}a_{J}-\tilde{a}_{I}\wedge{\rm d}\tilde{a}_{J}\right)+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge({\rm d}c^{I}-F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}})

(4.54)

\displaystyle+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}\left(a_{I}\wedge{\rm d}c^{I}-\tilde{a}_{I}\wedge F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)-\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}\tilde{b}_{I}\wedge(F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}-{\rm d}c^{I})\bigg]\,,

where
c^{I}
represents the world-volume remnant of the bulk gauge field enclosed by the two interfaces. Integrating it out sets
\tilde{b}_{I}=-b_{I}-a
. Furthermore, integrating out
b_{I}
imposes
F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}=F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}
, so that we can just set
F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}=F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}\equiv F^{I}
. Then, redefining
a_{I}\rightarrow a_{I}+\tilde{a}_{I}
one gets

{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathbbm{1})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)=\,\int Da\,D\tilde{a}\;{\rm exp}\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\mathsf{N}^{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}a_{I}\wedge{\rm d}a_{J}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}a_{I}\wedge{\rm d}\tilde{a}_{J}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}a_{I}\wedge F^{I}\right]\,.

(4.55)

This is the natural generalization to
n\geq 1
of the condensation defect found in
[
66
]
, and can be interpreted as a
‘one-gauging’
[
118
]
of a discrete magnetic one-form symmetry group
\Gamma^{*(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}\subset[U(1)^{(1)}_{\rm m}]^{n}
.

More explicitly, by
integrating out
\tilde{a}_{I}
one gets the constraints
{\rm d}a_{I}=0
and
\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{I}a_{J}\in 2\pi H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. That is, the closed one-forms
\eta_{I}\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{I}a_{J}
can be regarded as the integral uplift of a
\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{N}}
cohomology class
\bm{\eta}\in H^{1}(X,\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{N}})
. We can then rewrite the condensation defect as follows

{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathbbm{1})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)=\frac{1}{\left|H^{0}(\Sigma,\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{N}})\right|}\sum_{\bm{\eta}\in H^{1}(\Sigma,\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{N}})}\exp\left[{\rm i}(\mathsf{N}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}\oint_{\Sigma}\eta_{I}\cup F^{J}\right]\exp\left[\pi{\rm i}\delta^{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}\eta_{I}\cup\beta_{J}(\bm{\eta})\right]\,,

(4.56)

where
\beta_{I}(\bm{\eta})=(\mathsf{N}^{-1})^{J}{}_{I}\delta\eta_{J}\in H^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})
gives the Bockstein image of
\bm{\eta}\in H^{1}(X,\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{N}})
. Notice that the last factor in (
4.56
) introduces a discrete torsion phase for this one-gauging.

5
Action on line defects

The action of the GZ defects
{\cal D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma)
on local operators can be immediately read from (
3.9
). For instance, suppose that
{\cal D}_{\cal S}(S^{3})
is inserted into a correlator, where
S^{3}
surrounds a local gauge-invariant operator
{\cal O}(x)
constructed out of
F^{I},G_{J}
and
\phi^{i}
, and the orientation is fixed so that the interior of
S^{3}
corresponds to the left side of
S^{3}
.
Collapsing
S^{3}
to a point leaves the operator
\tilde{\cal O}(x)
obtained by applying the GZ transformations (
2.9a
). So, the action on local operators is the same as that expected from a standard invertible operator.

The action of GZ defects on Wilson, ’t Hooft lines and more general dyonic line operators is more interesting, and will further highlight the non-invertible nature of these defects. It is convenient to directly consider a general dyonic line.

\displaystyle L_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma)=\exp{\left({\rm i}\,\tilde{\ell}^{I}\oint_{\gamma}\tilde{A}_{I}-{\rm i}\,\ell_{I}\oint_{\gamma}A^{I}\right)}\,,

(5.1)

where
\tilde{A}_{I}
are the S-dual gauge fields, such that
{\rm d}\tilde{A}_{I}=G_{I}
, and we have introduced the
2n
-dimensional vector

\mathbbm{L}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{\ell}^{I}\\
{\ell}_{J}\end{array}\right)\,,

(5.2)

which is formed by the magnetic and electric charges of the dyonic line. These charges must be integral for (
5.1
) to be invariant under large gauge transformations of
A^{I}
and
\tilde{A}_{I}
. The signs in (
5.1
) are chosen for later convenience. As particular subcases, by setting
\tilde{\ell}^{I}=0
or
\ell_{I}=0
,
L_{\mathbbm{L}}
reduces to a Wilson or ’t Hooft lines,

W_{\bm{\ell}}(\gamma)\equiv\exp{\left(-{\rm i}\,\ell_{I}\oint_{\gamma}A^{I}\right)}\quad,\quad H_{\tilde{\bm{\ell}}}(\gamma)\equiv\exp{\left({\rm i}\,\tilde{\ell}^{I}\oint_{\gamma}\tilde{A}_{I}\right)}\,,

(5.3)

where
\bm{\ell}
and
\tilde{\bm{\ell}}
denote the charge vectors of components
\ell_{I}
and
\tilde{\ell}^{I}
, respectively.

The action of the GZ defects on these line operators is cleaner if we restrict ourselves to homologically trivial curves
\gamma
, which can hence be written as
\gamma=\partial M_{2}
for some two-dimensional surface
M_{2}
. In this case (
5.1
) can be rewritten as

\widehat{L}_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma,M_{2})=\exp\left({\rm i}\,\tilde{\ell}^{I}\int_{M_{2}}G_{I}-{\rm i}\,\ell_{I}\int_{M_{2}}F^{I}\right)\equiv\exp\left({\rm i}\,\int_{M_{2}}\mathbb{L}^{\rm t}\Omega\mathbbm{F}\right)\,,

(5.4)

and we can correspondingly denote the purely electric and magnetic subcases as

\widehat{W}_{\bm{\ell}}(\gamma,M_{2})\equiv\exp{\left(-{\rm i}\,\ell_{I}\int_{M_{2}}F^{I}\right)}\quad,\quad\widehat{H}_{\tilde{\bm{\ell}}}(\gamma,M_{2})\equiv\exp{\left({\rm i}\,\tilde{\ell}^{I}\int_{M_{2}}G_{I}\right)}\,.

(5.5)

For these operators we use a different symbol since they actually make sense for any, not necessarily integral, value of the charges
\ell_{I}
and
\tilde{\ell}^{J}
. Only for integral charges do (
5.4
) correspond to the genuine line operators (
5.1
), and are completely independent of the choice of
M_{2}
:

\widehat{L}_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma,M_{2})\equiv L_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma)\quad~~~\Leftrightarrow\quad~~~\tilde{\ell}^{I},\ell_{J}\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(5.6)

Otherwise, (
5.4
) must be interpreted as open surface operators, which depend on both
\gamma
and the relative homology class of
M_{2}
.

The action of a topological GZ defect
{\cal D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma)
on the surface operators (
5.4
) immediately follows from the gluing conditions described by (
3.9
). Suppose that we first put
\widehat{L}_{\mathbbm{L}}(M_{2})
on the right of
\Sigma
. Hence, if we sweep
{\cal D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma)
past
\widehat{L}_{\mathbbm{L}}(M_{2})
, the latter will undergo a transformation

\widehat{L}_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma,M_{2})=\exp\left({\rm i}\,\int_{M_{2}}\mathbb{L}^{\rm t}\Omega\mathbbm{F}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\quad\longrightarrow\quad\widehat{L}_{{\cal S}\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma,M_{2})=\exp\left({\rm i}\,\int_{M_{2}}({\cal S}\mathbb{L})^{\rm t}\Omega\mathbbm{F}_{\text{\tiny L}}\right)\,,

(5.7)

where we have used the fact that
\mathbbm{F}_{\text{\tiny R}}\rightarrow{\cal S}^{-1}\mathbbm{F}_{\text{\tiny L}}
across the defect, and the defining identity (
2.10
) rewritten as
\Omega{\cal S}^{-1}={\cal S}^{\rm t}\Omega
. In other words, sweeping
{\cal D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma)
past a surface operator (
5.4
), from the left to the right, turns it into a new surface operator with charge vector
\mathbbm{L}^{\prime}={\cal S}\mathbbm{L}
. More explicitly, using the decomposition (
2.23
), the new charges are

\tilde{\ell}^{\prime I}=\mathsf{A}^{I}{}_{J}\tilde{\ell}^{I}+\mathsf{B}^{IJ}\ell_{J}\quad,\quad\ell^{\prime}_{I}=\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\tilde{\ell}^{I}+\mathsf{D}_{I}{}^{J}\ell_{J}\,.

(5.8)

It is now clear that a GZ defect corresponding to a
non
-integral symplectic matrix
{\cal S}\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Q})
generically turns a genuine dyonic line (
5.1
) into a non-genuine one,

L_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma)\quad~~~\longrightarrow\quad~~~\widehat{L}_{{\cal S}\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma,M_{2})\,,

(5.9)

see Figure
2
.
Of course, this is not always the case as, for any choice of rational matrices appearing in (
2.23
), one can always find charges
\tilde{\ell}^{I},\ell_{J}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the new charges (
5.8
) are integral too.

\mathcal{D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma)
L_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma)
\mathcal{D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma^{\prime})
\hat{L}_{{\cal S}\mathbb{L}}(\gamma,M_{2})

Figure 2
:
Action of the defect
\mathcal{D}_{{\cal S}}
on the line operator
L_{\mathbb{L}}
.

Suppose for instance that a GZ model admits an element
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, with
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}
as in (
2.24
), for some
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then (
5.9
) implies that a Wilson line
W_{\bm{\ell}}(\gamma)
– see (
5.3
) – is transparent to
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
. On the other hand, by sweeping
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
past a ’t Hooft line
H_{\tilde{\bm{\ell}}}(\gamma)
from the left to the right, one gets the open surface operators

H_{\tilde{\bm{\ell}}}(\gamma)\widehat{W}_{\mathsf{C}\tilde{\bm{\ell}}}(\gamma,M_{2})=H_{\tilde{\bm{\ell}}}(\gamma)\exp\left(-{\rm i}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\tilde{\ell}^{I}\int_{M_{2}}F^{J}\right)\,.

(5.10)

Analogously, if a GZ model admits element an
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
of the form (
2.24
), then sweeping the corresponding topological defect
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)
past
W_{\bm{\ell}}(\gamma)
and
H_{\tilde{\bm{\ell}}}(\gamma)
turns them into the open surface operators

\widehat{W}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}\bm{\ell}}(\gamma)=\exp\left[-{\rm i}(\mathsf{A}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}\ell_{I}\int_{M_{2}}F^{J}\right]\quad,\quad\widehat{H}_{\mathsf{A}\tilde{\bm{\ell}}}(\gamma)=\exp\left({\rm i}\mathsf{A}^{I}{}_{J}\tilde{\ell}^{J}\int_{M_{2}}G_{I}\right)\,,

(5.11)

respectively.

Something even more interesting happens if the homology class of
\gamma
is non-trivial. In this case the line operators (
5.1
) are still well defined, while the surface operators (
5.4
) are not, so that the transition (
5.9
) appears obstructed. However, one can allow
M_{2}
to end on
\Sigma
– see Figure
3
. Indeed, if
\gamma
is contained in the right region of a tubolar neighborhood of
\Sigma
, we can assume it to be homologous to a curve
\gamma^{\prime}\subset\Sigma
, and we can take
M_{2}
such that
\partial M_{2}=\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}
. In this case (
5.9
) can still be realized and would describe a transition from a genuine line operator to a non-genuine ‘disorder’ line operator, which is linked to the topological defect by the surface
M_{2}
.

\mathcal{D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma)
L_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma)
\mathcal{D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma^{\prime})
\hat{L}_{{\cal S}\mathbb{L}}(M_{2})
\gamma^{\prime}

Figure 3
:
Action of the defect
\mathcal{D}_{{\cal S}}
on the line operator
L_{\mathbb{L}}
.

These types of effect are one of the hallmarks of non-invertible symmetries.
We will further expand upon them in Section
6
, which focuses on a simple
n=1
GZ model and whose main conclusions can be immediately extended to more complicated GZ models – see also
[
66
,
23
,
118
,
122
,
123
]
for similar results that are more directly connected with our
n=1
examples.

6
Examples with one gauge field

So far the discussion has been rather general. In order to better illustrate it, in this section we apply our general results to two simple concrete models with
n=1
, i.e. involving only a single U(1) gauge field
A
. The first one is the axion-Maxwell theory, whose generalized symmetry structure has already been thoroughly studied in the literature
[
66
,
23
,
122
,
124
,
125
,
126
]
. It will provide a simple warm-up example, which will allow us to show how our framework reproduces some known results
[
66
,
23
]
, without dwelling much on them. The second model is an immediate generalization of the first one, which includes a dilaton and has GZ symmetry group
\mathscr{G}={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. The neutral sector is enlarged to include a dilaton, and can be represented by a complex field
\tau
parametrizing the coset SL
(2,\mathbb{R})/{\rm U}(1)
. We will therefore refer to this model as the
\tau
-Maxwell model. We will discuss the construction of GZ topological defects
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
with
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{Q})
, and study some of their properties in details.

6.1
Warm-up: the axion-Maxwell model

The axion-Maxwell theory is defined by the Lagrangian

{\cal L}=-\frac{1}{2g^{2}}F\wedge*F-\frac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{f}^{2}{\rm d}\vartheta\wedge*{\rm d}\vartheta-\frac{\vartheta}{8\pi^{2}}F\wedge F\,,

(6.1)

where
\mathsf{f}
is some mass scale.
This is probably the simplest realization of the GZ general setting reviewed in Section
2
, with one gauge field (
n=1
) and neutral sector provided by
\vartheta
.
In this case the action (
2.9a
) of the GZ group
\mathscr{G}
reduces to a constant axion shift

\vartheta\rightarrow f_{\mathsf{c}}(\vartheta)=\vartheta-2\pi\mathsf{c}\,,

(6.2)

with
\mathsf{c}\in\mathbb{R}
.
The dual field strength can be easily computed by applying (
2.3
):

G=-\frac{2\pi}{g^{2}}*F-\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}F\,.

(6.3)

It is clear that under (
6.2
), the doublet (
2.5
) formed by
F
and
G
transforms as in (
2.9b
), with

{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}1&0\\
\mathsf{c}&1\end{array}\right)\in{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\,.

(6.4)

Furthermore, we get a simple realization of the GZ condition (
2.14
), with

{\cal L}_{{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}}\equiv{\cal L}_{{\mathsf{C}}}={\cal L}+\frac{\mathsf{c}}{4\pi}F\wedge F\,,

(6.5)

which is just a simple subcase of (
2.26
). Here and in the following, we use the lowercase letter
\mathsf{c}
, rather than
\mathsf{C}
, to emphasize that it is a number rather than a matrix. Clearly
{\cal L}
and
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{C}}
are physically equivalent only if
\mathsf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}
(on spin manifolds), and only in this case does the GZ transformation correspond to a standard zero-form symmetry of the theory. In our general notation, these integral shifts identify the integral subgroup
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. We may quotient (or gauge) this group, i.e. by assuming that the axion is periodic,
\vartheta\simeq\vartheta+2\pi
, but from a purely QFT viewpoint this is not compulsory.

Let us now restrict to the rational GZ subgroup
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, corresponding to shifts (
6.2
) with
\mathsf{c}\in\mathbb{Q}
. We can then set

\mathsf{c}=\frac{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}\,.

(6.6)

This is the
n=1
form of the factorization (
4.25
), and again we use lower case letters since we are dealing with numbers rather than matrices.
Following Section
3
, the GZ topological defects
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
for rational values (
6.6
) of
\mathsf{c}
can be obtained by fusing the corresponding
{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
defects and
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
interfaces as in Figure
1
. Let us then revisit the construction of these objects for this specific setting. We emphasize that the rest of this section is just a mild rephrasing, following our more general framework, of known results
[
66
,
23
]
.

Let us start with the
{\cal U}_{\mathsf{C}}
defect, which can actually be constructed for any real
\mathsf{c}
. We can write (
6.4
) as in (
3.2
) by taking

T=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}0&0\\
\mathsf{c}&0\end{array}\right)\in{\rm sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\,.

(6.7)

This corresponds to the infinitesimal transformation
\delta_{T}\vartheta=-2\pi{\mathsf{c}}
. These equations provide simple examples of (
1.1
) and (
2.2
), respectively. The corresponding current

{\cal J}_{T}=2\pi\mathsf{c}\,\mathsf{f}^{2}*{\rm d}\vartheta\,,

(6.8)

can be computed by applying (
2.19
). The axion equation of motion can be written as non-conservation equation

{\rm d}{\cal J}_{T}=\frac{{\mathsf{c}}}{4\pi}F\wedge F\,,

(6.9)

which matches the general formula (
2.21
).
With the choice (
6.6
), the defect (
3.4
) takes the form

{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)={\rm exp}\left(\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{f}^{2}\oint_{\Sigma}\,\,*{\rm d}\vartheta\right)

(6.10)

According to (
3.6
), it acts as follows,

\begin{split}&\quad~~~~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)}\\
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi_{\text{\tiny L}})&\qquad~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\quad\Bigg|}\qquad~~~~{\cal L}\left(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}-\frac{2\pi\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}\right)={\cal L}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})={\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})+\frac{\mathsf{p}}{4\pi\mathsf{n}}F\wedge F\,.\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{12873454}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}-2\pi\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{12873454}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}-2\pi\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{15706961}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}-2\pi\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.8499pt}{\resizebox{20075139}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.67657pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}-2\pi\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}}\end{split}

(6.11)

More directly, the
\mathcal{U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
defect in (
6.11
) can also be obtained by integrating the trivial operator
-{\rm d}{\cal J}+\frac{\mathsf{p}}{4\pi\sf n}F\wedge F=0
on the right of
\Sigma

[
66
]
.

Consider now the corresponding
{\cal W}
interface, focusing on the minimal ones, as in Section
4.3
. They can be defined by picking a factorization (
2.12
) that is
coprime
, i.e. such that
\gcd(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{n})=1
. This fixes
\mathsf{p}
and
\mathsf{n}
up to an overall sign. According to the general discussion, this sign does not affect the definition of minimal interface, and we can then remove this small degeneracy by imposing
\mathsf{n}>0
. Following
[
120
]
, one can then define a minimal three-dimensional TQFT, realizing a
\mathbb{Z}^{(1)}_{\mathsf{n}}
one-form symmetry with anomaly fixed by
\mathsf{p}
mod
\mathsf{n}
, which can be detected by coupling it to a background
\mathbb{Z}^{(1)}_{\mathsf{n}}
gauge potential
{\cal B}
. Since coprime
\mathsf{n}>0
and
\mathsf{p}
are uniquely fixed by
\mathsf{c}
, the corresponding partition function
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\Sigma}[{\cal B}]
depends just on
\mathsf{c}
. Our general formula (
4.23
) with the choice (
4.52
) becomes

{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)={\cal I}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]\int Db\;\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge(F_{\text{\tiny R}}-F_{\text{\tiny L}})\right]\,,

(6.12)

with
16
16

16

Since the anomaly of the TQFT is fixed by
\mathsf{p}
mod
\mathsf{n}
, in order to ensure the gluing condition
G_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=G_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}+\frac{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}F_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
, the partition function
\mathcal{{\cal I}}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]
generically includes also a possible counterterm of the form
\exp\left(-\frac{{\rm i}\mathsf{z}}{4\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}A_{\text{\tiny R}}\wedge F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)
, with
\mathsf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}
– cf. the discussion around (
4.35
).

\mathcal{{\cal I}}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]={\cal A}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\Sigma}\left[F_{\text{\tiny R}}/\mathsf{n}\right]\,.

(6.13)

As a simple subcase, suppose that
\mathsf{p}=1
. Then
\mathcal{A}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\Sigma}\left[F_{\text{\tiny R}}/\mathsf{n}\right]
reduces to the
U(1)_{\mathsf{n}}
Chern-Simons theory coupled to
F_{\text{\tiny R}}
, so that

{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)=\int Da\,Db\;{\rm exp}\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}\left[\frac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{n}\,a\wedge{\rm d}a+a\wedge F_{\text{\tiny R}}+b\wedge(F_{\text{\tiny R}}-F_{\text{\tiny L}})\right]\right)\,.

(6.14)

More generically,
\mathcal{A}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\Sigma}\left[F_{\text{\tiny R}}/\mathsf{n}\right]
can be defined in terms of the four-dimensional description (
4.44
) of the minimal three-dimensional TQFT. In the present case this reduces to

-\frac{\mathsf{n}\mathsf{p}}{4\pi}\int_{Y}B\wedge B+\frac{\mathsf{n}}{2\pi}\int_{Y}B\wedge\tilde{F}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}\tilde{A}\wedge F_{\text{\tiny R}}\,,

(6.15)

with
Y
such that
\partial Y=\Sigma
. As in (
3.7
), the interface
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
separates two
inequivalent theories, rather than implementing a transformation within a given theory:

\begin{split}&\quad~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}\mathcal{W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)}\\
{\cal L}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})={\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})+\frac{\mathsf{p}}{4\pi\mathsf{n}}F\wedge F&\qquad~{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}\quad\Bigg|}\qquad~~~~{\cal L}\left(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\,.\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{13978706}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{13978706}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{17053553}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.8499pt}{\resizebox{21758849}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.67657pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}}\end{split}

(6.16)

We can now fuse (
6.10
) and (
6.12
) as in (
3.1
), obtaining the GZ topological defect

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle={\cal U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)\times{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)

(6.17)

\displaystyle=\,\mathcal{{\cal I}}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]\int Db\;\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge(F_{\text{\tiny R}}-F_{\text{\tiny L}})\right]\times{\rm exp}\left(\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{f}^{2}\oint_{\Sigma}\,\,*{\rm d}\vartheta\right)\,,

which acts as follows

\begin{split}&\quad~~~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)}\\
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})&\quad~~~~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\quad\Bigg|}\qquad~~~~~~~~{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}})\quad~~~~~~~~~.\\
&\,\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{16690048}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}+2\pi\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{16690048}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}+2\pi\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{20089465}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}+2\pi\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.8499pt}{\resizebox{25293729}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.67657pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\vartheta_{\text{\tiny R}}+2\pi\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}}\end{split}

(6.18)

Hence
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
realizes the complete GZ transformations defined by (
6.2
) and (
6.4
), with
\mathsf{c}
as in (
6.6
). As anticipated above, the GZ defect
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
coincides with the non-invertible topological defect already found in
[
66
,
23
]
. This topological defect can also be obtained through a half-space gauging procedure
[
66
]
, which can be regarded as a particular subcase of the half-space gauging discussed in Appendix
E.3
.

6.2
\tau
-Maxwell model

Let us now turn to the
\tau
-Maxwell model, which provides a simple but non-trivial generalization of the axion-Maxwell model discussed above. We still have just one gauge field
A
, while the neutral sector is provided by a dynamical complexified coupling

\tau=\frac{\vartheta}{2\pi}+{\rm i}e^{-\phi}\,,

(6.19)

parametrizing the complex upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}=\{{\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau>0\}
. The Lagrangian is
17
17

17

The Lagrangian (
6.20
) is a particular case of (
A.1
), with
n=1
and
{\cal N}=-\tau
.

\displaystyle{\cal L}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi}{\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau\,F\wedge*F-\frac{1}{4\pi}{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau\,F\wedge F-\mathsf{f}^{2}\,\frac{{\rm d}\tau\wedge*{\rm d}\bar{\tau}}{({\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau)^{2}}\,,

(6.20)

where
\mathsf{f}
is a mass scale. Furthermore, for the moment we are not considering any discrete identification in
\mathbb{H}
.

The kinetic term of
\tau
corresponds to a hyperbolic metric on
\mathbb{H}
, which can be also written as the metric on the coset
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})/{\rm U}(1)
. Its isometry group
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\simeq{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
can be identified with the GZ symmetry group
\mathscr{G}
of the model, which therefore coincides with the entire symplectic group:

{\cal S}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\mathsf{a}&\mathsf{b}\\
\mathsf{c}&\mathsf{d}\end{array}\right)\in\mathscr{G}={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}-\mathsf{b}\mathsf{c}=1\,.

(6.21)

This representation of
{\cal S}
is the analogue of (
2.23
), and as in Section
6.1
we use lowercase letters since they are numbers rather than matrices.
The classical GZ transformation associated with any
{\cal S}
of the form (
6.21
) acts
on
\tau
and on the doublet

\mathbbm{F}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}F\\
G\end{array}\right)

(6.22)

as in (
2.9
), with:

f_{\cal S}(\tau)=\frac{\mathsf{d}\tau-\mathsf{c}}{\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{b}\tau}\quad,\quad{\cal S}\mathbbm{F}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\mathsf{a}&\mathsf{b}\\
\mathsf{c}&\mathsf{d}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}F\\
G\end{array}\right)\,.

(6.23)

The explicit form of dual field strength
G
can be obtained from (
2.3
):

G=-{\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau\,*F-{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau\,F\,.

(6.24)

The generic infinitesimal GZ transformations (
2.2
) and (
2.6
) now take the form

T=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\mathsf{u}&\mathsf{v}\\
\mathsf{w}&-\mathsf{u}\end{array}\right)\in{\rm sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\quad,\quad\delta_{T}\tau=\xi_{T}(\tau)=-\mathsf{w}-2\mathsf{u}\tau+\mathsf{v}\tau^{2}\,,

(6.25)

and the associated GZ current (
2.19
) becomes

{\cal J}_{T}=-\mathsf{f}^{2}\,\frac{\xi_{T}(\tau)*{\rm d}\bar{\tau}+\bar{\xi}_{T}(\bar{\tau})*{\rm d}\tau}{({\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau)^{2}}\,,

(6.26)

while its non-conservation equation (
2.21
) reads

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\cal J}_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\mathsf{w}F\wedge F-\mathsf{v}G\wedge G-2\mathsf{u}F\wedge G\right)\,.

(6.27)

Since the GZ assumptions are satisfied, according to our general discussion we should be able to construct a topological defect
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
implementing any GZ transformation
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}={\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{Q})={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{Q})
. The general claim exploits the fact that any element of
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Q})
can be decomposed as the product of elements of the form (
2.24
). Furthermore, we can use the integral factorizations (
4.2
) and (
4.25
). In the present
n=1
setting, the generators (
2.24
) of
{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{Q})={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{Q})
can then be written as
18
18

18

In fact, any
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}
may be decomposed into a product of elements of the type
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
{\cal S}_{\Omega}
, and therefore we may focus on latter. It is nevertheless interesting to discuss a direct construction of
\mathsf{A}
defects.

{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\mathsf{m}}{\mathsf{e}}&0\\
0&\frac{\mathsf{e}}{\mathsf{m}}\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\frac{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}&1\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad{\cal S}_{\Omega}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)\,,

(6.28)

with no loss of generality, we can assume
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{n},\mathsf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
\mathsf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
19
19

19

We can assume
\mathsf{m}/\mathsf{e}>0
since a negative value can be obtained by taking the product of
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
{\cal S}^{2}_{\Omega}=-\mathbbm{1}
.

As discussed in Sections
4.2
and
4.3
, minimal defects correspond to coprime factorizations, which in the present model means
{\rm gcd}(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})=1
and
{\rm gcd}(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})=1
.

As already emphasized, in the
\tau
-Maxwell model the rational GZ group
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
coincides with the entire rational symplectic group
{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore, there should exist a GZ topological defect
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
for any of the three transformations (
6.28
). We can then separately discuss each of them.

6.2.1
\mathsf{A}
defect

We first consider
\mathsf{A}
GZ topological defects, associated with
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}
in (
6.28
).
Following the general procedure of Section
3
, an
\mathsf{A}
topological defect can be written in the form (
3.1
), that is

{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)={\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)\times{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)\,.

(6.29)

The defect
{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)
implements the transformation
\tau\rightarrow\frac{\mathsf{e}^{2}}{\mathsf{m}^{2}}\tau
and can be written in the form (
3.6
). In order to do that, we need to find
T
such that
\exp T={\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}
. It is easy to see that we must take
T
as in (
6.25
), with
\mathsf{u}=\log(\mathsf{m}/\mathsf{e})
and
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{w}=0
. By using the corresponding current (
6.26
) in (
3.4
) we get

{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)={\rm exp}\left[4{\rm i}\mathsf{f}^{2}\log\left(\frac{\mathsf{m}}{\mathsf{e}}\right)\oint_{\Sigma}\,\frac{{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\left(\bar{\tau}*{\rm d}\tau\right)}{({\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau)^{2}}\right]\,.

(6.30)

The interface
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
can be obtained as
n=1
subcase of the more general interface (
4.3
). Namely,

{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)=\int Db\,\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge\left(\mathsf{e}F_{\text{\tiny L}}-\mathsf{m}F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\right]\,,

(6.31)

where
b
is a world-volume U(1) gauge field defined on
\Sigma
.
This interface was first introduced in
[
117
]
for studying non-invertible symmetries of pure electromagnetism with rational fine-structure constant. According to the general property (
3.7
), it separates a theory defined by

{\cal L}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})\equiv{\cal L}\left(\frac{\mathsf{e}}{\mathsf{m}}F_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}}\right)\,,

(6.32)

and
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})
, with gluing conditions
\mathsf{e}F_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=\mathsf{m}F_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
and
\mathsf{m}G_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=\mathsf{e}G_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
. These can also been obtained from (
6.31
), by integrating out
b
and from the boundary contributions to the saddle point conditions for
A_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
A_{\text{\tiny R}}
.

In the present model, the general GZ condition (
2.14
) boils down to
{\cal L}(F,\frac{\mathsf{e}^{2}}{\mathsf{m}^{2}}\tau)={\cal L}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(F,\tau)
, which can be easily verified. The action of
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
defined as in (
6.29
) can then be obtained by concatenating
{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
as in (
3.8
):

\begin{split}&\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)}\\
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})\quad~~~~&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\quad\Bigg|}\quad~~~~~~~{\cal L}\left(F_{\text{\tiny I}},\frac{\mathsf{e}^{2}}{\mathsf{m}^{2}}\tau_{\text{\tiny I}}\right)={\cal L}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(F_{\text{\tiny I}},\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})\quad~\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\scriptstyle{(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},(\mathsf{e}/\mathsf{m})^{2}\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma}}}\end{split}\quad\begin{split}&\;\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}\quad\Bigg|}\quad~~~~~~{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})\,,\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}\scriptstyle{(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}=((\mathsf{m}/\mathsf{e})F_{\text{\tiny R}},(\mathsf{e}/\mathsf{m})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}}\end{split}

(6.33)

By fusing
{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
we conclude that the GZ topological defect

\mathcal{D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)={\rm exp}\left[4{\rm i}\mathsf{f}^{2}\log\left(\frac{\mathsf{m}}{\mathsf{e}}\right)\oint_{\Sigma}\,\frac{{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\left(\bar{\tau}*{\rm d}\tau\right)}{({\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau)^{2}}\right]\times\int Db\,\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge\left(\mathsf{e}F_{\text{\tiny L}}-\mathsf{m}F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\right]

(6.34)

induces the complete GZ transformation (
6.23
) associated with
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}
in (
6.28
):

\begin{split}&\quad~~~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)}\\
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})&\quad~~~~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\quad\Bigg|}\qquad~~~~~~~~{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})\quad~~~~~~~~~.\\
&\,\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=\big((\mathsf{m}/\mathsf{e})F_{\text{\tiny R}},(\mathsf{e}/\mathsf{m})G_{\text{\tiny R}},(\mathsf{e}/\mathsf{m})^{2}\tau_{\text{\tiny R}}\big)|_{\Sigma}}}\end{split}

(6.35)

Note that for
\mathsf{e}\neq 1
or
\mathsf{m}\neq 1
this topological defect is non-invertible. We will come back to this point in Section
6.2.4
. We also observe that
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},1)}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal D}^{(1,\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}={\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
– cf. the more general (
4.7
) – and that
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},1)}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
{\cal D}^{(1,\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
can be obtained from the half-space gauging procedures described in Appendix
D.1
and
D.2
, respectively.

6.2.2
\mathsf{C}
defect

We now turn to the
\sf C
defects which, recalling again (
3.1
), can be written as

{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)={\cal U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)\times{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)\,.

(6.36)

They are almost identical to the
\mathsf{C}
defects constructed for the axion-Maxwell model in Section
6.1
. The only minor difference is the form of the current
{\cal J}_{T}
that one has to use in (
3.4
). We can still take
T
as in (
6.7
), with
\mathsf{c}=\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n}
. This means that in (
6.25
) we must take
\mathsf{w}=\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n}
and
\mathsf{u}=\mathsf{v}=0
. So, from (
6.26
) it follows that we now have

{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)={\rm exp}\left[\frac{2{\rm i}\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{f}^{2}\oint_{\Sigma}\,\frac{*{\rm d}{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau}{({\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau)^{2}}\right]\,.

(6.37)

The interface
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
can be chosen as in (
6.12
). Hence, fusing it with (
6.37
) as in (
6.36
), we get the corresponding GZ topological defect

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{{\cal I}}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]\int Db\;\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge(F_{\text{\tiny R}}-F_{\text{\tiny L}})\right]

(6.38)

\displaystyle\times{\rm exp}\left[\frac{2{\rm i}\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{f}^{2}\oint_{\Sigma}\,\frac{*{\rm d}{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau}{({\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau)^{2}}\right]\,.

By concatenating
{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
, it is easy to see that
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
acts as follows on the bulk theory

\begin{split}&\quad~~~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)}\\
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})&\quad~~~~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\quad\Bigg|}\qquad~~~~~~~~{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})\,,\\
&\,\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{15485516}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}}+\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{15485516}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}}+\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{18674127}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}}+\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.8499pt}{\resizebox{23560389}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.67657pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny R}},G_{\text{\tiny R}}+(\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}}+\mathsf{p}/\mathsf{n})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}}\end{split}

(6.39)

and hence realizes the complete GZ transformation (
6.23
) associated to
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}
appearing in (
6.28
).

As in axion-Maxwell, the interface
{\cal W}_{\mathsf{C}}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}
– and hence the GZ topological defect
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{C}}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}
– can be obtained through the half-space gauging procedure discussed in
[
66
]
, which corresponds to the
n=1
subcase of the more general half-space gauging discussed in Appendix
E.3
. Furthermore, if
\mathsf{n}\neq 1
,
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{C}}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}
is non-invertible. This will be made more explicit in Section
6.2.4
.

6.2.3
\Omega
defect

We finally consider the
\Omega
defect
{\cal D}_{\Omega}
. Using again the decomposition (
3.1
), this can be written in the form

{\cal D}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)={\cal U}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)\times{\cal W}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)\,.

(6.40)

By picking
T
in (
6.25
) such that
\Omega=\exp T
, and using the corresponding current (
6.26
), we can construct
{\cal U}_{\Omega}
as in (
3.4
). More explicitly, in (
6.25
) we must take
\mathsf{u}=0
and
\mathsf{v}=-\mathsf{w}=\frac{\pi}{2}
. Using these values in (
6.26
) we get

{\cal U}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)=\exp\left[-{\rm i}\pi\mathsf{f}^{2}\,{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\oint_{\Sigma}\frac{\left(1+\tau^{2}\right)*{\rm d}\bar{\tau}}{({\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau)^{2}}\right]\,.

(6.41)

On the other hand, the
{\cal W}_{\Omega}
interface
[
112
,
113
,
114
]
can be obtained by taking the case
n=1
of (
4.1c
),

{\cal W}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)=\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}A_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\,,

(6.42)

which separates the S-dual Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{\Omega}
and
{\cal L}
– see Appendix
B.3
. The action of
{\cal D}_{\Omega}
can then be obtained by concatenating
{\cal U}_{\Omega}
and
{\cal D}_{\Omega}
as in (
3.8
):

\begin{split}&\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{U}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)}\\
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})\quad~~~~~~&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\quad\Bigg|}\quad~~~~~~~~~{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny I}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})={\cal L}_{\Omega}(F_{\text{\tiny I}},\phi_{\text{\tiny I}})\quad~~~\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{11107699}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{11107699}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{13714118}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.8499pt}{\resizebox{17756525}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.67657pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}}\end{split}\quad\begin{split}&\;{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}\mathcal{W}_{\Omega}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}\quad\Bigg|}\quad~~~~~~~~{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\phi_{\text{\tiny R}})\,,\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.3,0.57}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{10584658}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}=(G_{\text{\tiny R}},-F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{10584658}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}=(G_{\text{\tiny R}},-F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.9706pt}{\resizebox{13184888}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.81111pt}{$\scriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}=(G_{\text{\tiny R}},-F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-5.74072pt}{\resizebox{17191163}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny I}},G_{\text{\tiny I}},\tau_{\text{\tiny I}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}=(G_{\text{\tiny R}},-F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}$}}}}}}\end{split}

(6.43)

The topological defect (
6.40
) is then given

{\cal D}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)=\exp\left(-{\rm i}\pi\mathsf{f}^{2}\,{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\oint_{\Sigma}\frac{\left(1+\tau^{2}\right)*{\rm d}\bar{\tau}}{({\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}\tau)^{2}}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}A_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\,,

(6.44)

which acts on the bulk theory by implementing the complete GZ transformation (
6.23
) defined by
{\cal S}_{\Omega}
:

\begin{split}&\quad~~~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)}\\
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})&\quad~~~~~~~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\quad\Bigg|}\qquad~~~~~~~~{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})\,.\\
&\,\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{11936449}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(G_{\text{\tiny R}},-F_{\text{\tiny R}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{11936449}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(G_{\text{\tiny R}},-F_{\text{\tiny R}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.87036pt}{\resizebox{14829374}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(G_{\text{\tiny R}},-F_{\text{\tiny R}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.8499pt}{\resizebox{19267722}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.67657pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(F_{\text{\tiny L}},G_{\text{\tiny L}},\tau_{\text{\tiny L}})|_{\Sigma}=(G_{\text{\tiny R}},-F_{\text{\tiny R}},-1/\tau_{\text{\tiny R}})|_{\Sigma}$}}}}}}\end{split}

(6.45)

We emphasize that, differently from
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{C}
defects discussed above,
\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)
is invertible.

So far we have constructed the symmetry defects without explicitly discussing their ’t Hooft anomalies. For instance there should be a non-trivial ’t Hooft anomaly for some of the elements of the
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
group of invertible symmetries, along the lines of
[
110
]
. The anomaly shows up in the presence of a non-trivial background for the
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
zero-form symmetry.
In addition, as mentioned in Section
2
, in curved spacetimes one should also take into account the mixed anomaly between the
\Omega
transformation and gravity
[
106
]
. For our present purposes, it is not essential to explicitly consider this effect and therefore, for simplicity, we will restrict to (e.g. flat) spacetimes in which this anomaly is absent. Of course, in order to promote the gravitational background to a dynamical one, one should explictly take this effect into account.

6.2.4
Fusion of GZ topological defects

Let us now discuss some of the basic fusion rules of the GZ topological defects
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
,
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
{\cal D}_{\Omega}
– see (
6.34
), (
6.38
) and (
6.44
), respectively – leaving a more systematic investigation for future work. We observe that any of the fusion below relies on the general commutativity (
3.11
).

As already mentioned,
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{\Omega}}
is an invertible defect, in the sense that its fusion with
{\cal D}_{\Omega^{-1}}=\overline{{\cal D}}_{\Omega}
gives the identity. We also observe that, since
\Omega^{-1}=-\Omega
, the fusion rule of
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{\Omega}}
with itself produces the charge conjugation defect
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{-\mathbbm{1}}}
,

{\cal D}_{\mathsf{\Omega}}\times{\cal D}_{\mathsf{\Omega}}={\cal D}_{\mathsf{-\mathbbm{1}}}\,.

(6.46)

Another simple example is provided by:

{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e}^{\prime},\mathsf{m}^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{A}}={\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e}\mathsf{e}^{\prime},\mathsf{m}\mathsf{m}^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{A}}\quad~~~~~~\text{if}\quad~\gcd(\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e}^{\prime})=\gcd(\mathsf{m}^{\prime},\mathsf{e})=1\,.

(6.47)

The fusion of more general
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{A}}
defects will be discussed below. A less trivial example is provided by the self-fusion of a defect of the type (
6.38
) with
\mathsf{p}=1
and odd
\mathsf{n}
, which can be extracted from
[
122
,
116
]
:

{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)\times{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)=\mathcal{A}^{(\mathsf{n},2)}_{\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}/\mathsf{n}]\,{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},2)}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)\,.

(6.48)

More general fusions can be used to construct topological defects corresponding to more general GZ transformations. As simple examples, consider the following three types of
{\cal S}\in{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{Q})
matrices:

{\cal S}_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\mathsf{m}}{\mathsf{e}}&0\\
\frac{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}&\frac{\mathsf{e}}{\mathsf{m}}\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad{\cal S}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\frac{\mathsf{m}}{\mathsf{e}}\\
-\frac{\mathsf{e}}{\mathsf{m}}&0\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad{\cal S}_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&\frac{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}\end{array}\right)\,,

(6.49)

with
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
\mathsf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Corresponding GZ topological defects
{\cal D}_{1}
,
{\cal D}_{2}
and
{\cal D}_{3}
, implementing the respective GZ transformations (
6.23
), can be constructed as follows. (As already emphasized, a GZ transformation does not correspond to a unique GZ topological defect.)

Consider first
{\cal S}_{1}
. It can be factorized as

{\cal S}_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\mathsf{m}}{\mathsf{e}}&0\\
0&\frac{\mathsf{e}}{\mathsf{m}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\frac{\mathsf{m}\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{e}\mathsf{n}}&1\end{array}\right)\,.

(6.50)

Hence, an example of associated GZ defect is obtained by fusing the following
\mathsf{A}
and a
\mathsf{C}
defects:

{\cal D}_{1}\equiv{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e}\mathsf{n},\mathsf{m}\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}=\left({\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e}\mathsf{n},\mathsf{m}\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}\right)\times\left({\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal W}^{(\tilde{\mathsf{n}},\tilde{\mathsf{p}})}_{\mathsf{C}}\right)\,,

(6.51)

with
\tilde{\mathsf{p}}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
\tilde{\mathsf{p}}/\tilde{\mathsf{n}}=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{p}/(\mathsf{e}\mathsf{n})
and
\gcd(\tilde{\mathsf{n}},\tilde{\mathsf{p}})=1
.
In particular, the contribution of the resulting
{\cal W}
interface can be written more explicitly a

\displaystyle{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal W}^{(\tilde{\mathsf{n}},\tilde{\mathsf{p}})}_{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle={\cal I}^{(\tilde{\mathsf{n}},\tilde{\mathsf{p}})}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]\int Db\;{\rm exp}\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge\left(\mathsf{e}F_{\text{\tiny L}}-\mathsf{m}F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\right]\,.

(6.52)

We can then set
{\cal I}^{(\tilde{\mathsf{n}},\tilde{\mathsf{p}})}_{\mathsf{C},\Sigma}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}]={\cal A}_{\Sigma}^{(\tilde{\mathsf{n}},\tilde{\mathsf{p}})}[F_{\text{\tiny R}}/\tilde{\mathsf{n}}]
, or use a non-minimal TQFT, as the one defined in (
4.38
) for a general GZ model.

Next, consider the symplectic matrix
{\cal S}_{2}
, which can be written as

{\cal S}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\mathsf{m}}{\mathsf{e}}&0\\
0&\frac{\mathsf{e}}{\mathsf{m}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)\,.

(6.53)

Then, a corresponding GZ defect
{\cal D}_{2}
is given by

{\cal D}_{2}\equiv{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal D}_{\Omega}=\left({\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal U}_{\Omega}\right)\times\left({\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal W}_{\Omega}\right)\,,

(6.54)

with

{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal W}_{\Omega}=\int Da\,Db\,\;{\rm exp}\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge(\mathsf{e}F_{\text{\tiny L}}-\mathsf{m}{\rm d}a)+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}a\wedge F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]\,.

(6.55)

Finally,
{\cal S}_{3}
can be factorized as

{\cal S}_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\frac{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)\,.

(6.56)

and then a corresponding GZ defect
{\cal D}_{3}
can be defined as
