{\cal D}_{3}\equiv{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}\times{\cal D}_{\Omega}=\left({\cal U}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}\times{\cal U}_{\Omega}\right)\times\left({\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}\times{\cal W}_{\Omega}\right)\,.

(6.57)

Assuming
\gcd(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{n})=1
, the
{\cal W}
component can be written as

\displaystyle{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}\times{\cal W}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}[F_{\text{\tiny L}}/\mathsf{n}]\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}A_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F_{\text{\tiny R}}\,.\right)

(6.58)

Other GZ topological defects, corresponding to more general
{\cal S}\in{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{Q})
, can be obtained in a similar way. Namely, one can decompose
{\cal S}
into a product of elements of the form (
6.28
) and compute the ordered fusion of the associated GZ defects. Of course, the factorization is not unique and the resulting GZ defect
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
can a priori depend on the chosen factorization, as well as on the TQFT used to define the possible
\mathsf{C}
defects involved in the fusion. Hence, we again see how a given GZ tranformation does not correspond to a unique GZ defect. It would be extremely interesting to more systematically classify the possible GZ defects and, in particular, identify the possible “minimal” ones.

6.2.5
Condensation Defects

As already seen in Section
4
, if
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
is a non-invertible GZ defect, a fusion of the form
{\cal D}_{\cal S}\times\overline{{\cal D}}_{\cal S}
produces a non-trivial condensation defect. Let us discuss a few simple examples of such condensation defects and how they can also appear in more general fusions.

Take first the operator (
6.36
) with
\mathsf{p}=1
and
\mathsf{n}\geq 2
, and
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}
as in (
6.14
). The corresponding condensation defect is

{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}\equiv{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}\times\overline{{\cal D}}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}={\cal W}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}\times\overline{{\cal W}}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}\,.

(6.59)

This fusion can be straightforwardly computed, as done in Section
4.3.2
for the more general
\mathsf{C}
interface, and gives
the
n=1
subcase of the condensation defect (
4.55
)-(
4.56
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(\mathsf{n},1)}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle=\int Da\,D\tilde{a}\;{\rm exp}\left[\frac{{\rm i}\mathsf{n}}{4\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}a\wedge{\rm d}a+\frac{{\rm i}\mathsf{n}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}a\wedge{\rm d}\tilde{a}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}a\wedge F\right]

(6.60)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|H^{0}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{n}})|}\sum_{\eta\in H^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{n}})}\exp\left({\rm i}\oint_{\Sigma}\eta\cup F\right)\exp\left[\pi{\rm i}\mathsf{n}\oint_{\Sigma}\eta\cup\beta(\eta)\right]\,.

This coincides with the condensation defect found in
[
66
]
– see also
[
122
,
127
,
116
,
118
]
–
and can be interpreted as coming from a one-gauging of the finite magnetic one-form symmetry group
\mathbb{Z}^{(1)}_{\mathsf{n}}\subset U(1)^{(1)}_{\rm m}
. Notice that the discrete torsion phase in (
6.60
) gives a non-trivial effect only if
\mathsf{n}
is even
[
66
]
.

Let us now consider the condensation defect
associated with the GZ defect (
6.34
):

\displaystyle{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times\overline{{\cal D}}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}={\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e})}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}}

(6.61)

We can then proceed as explained for the general case above (
4.9
), namely, concatenating
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
{\cal W}^{(\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e})}_{\mathsf{C}}
, rewriting the enclosed bulk gauge field as a world-volume gauge field
a
, and making a simple field redefinition. As a result, we can write (
6.61
) as

{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}=\int Db\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}\mathsf{e}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge(F_{\text{\tiny L}}-F_{\text{\tiny R}})\right]\int DaD\tilde{b}\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}\left(\mathsf{m}\,\tilde{b}\wedge{\rm d}a-\mathsf{e}\,\tilde{b}\wedge F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\right]\,.

(6.62)

Assuming that
\gcd(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})=1
and adapting the general results of Section
4.2
, one concludes that
{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
can be rewritten as

{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}={\cal C}^{(\mathsf{e},1)}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal C}^{(1,\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\,,

(6.63)

with

\displaystyle{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{e},1)}_{\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|H^{0}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})|}\sum_{C\in H_{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})}{\rm exp}\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{\mathsf{e}}\oint_{C}G_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\,,

(6.64)

\displaystyle{\cal C}^{(1,\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|H^{0}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{m}})|}\sum_{\tilde{C}\in H_{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{m}})}{\rm exp}\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{\mathsf{m}}\oint_{\tilde{C}}F_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\,.

{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
coincides with the condensation defect found in
[
117
]
– see also
[
116
,
123
]
.

Finally, consider the more general fusion

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e}^{\prime},\mathsf{m}^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle={\cal U}^{(\mathsf{e}\mathsf{e}^{\prime},\mathsf{m}\mathsf{m}^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times\int Db\,D\tilde{b}\,Da\;{\rm exp}\Big[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b\wedge(\mathsf{e}F_{\text{\tiny L}}-\mathsf{m}{\rm d}a)

(6.65)

\displaystyle+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}\tilde{b}\wedge(\mathsf{e}^{\prime}{\rm d}a-\mathsf{m}^{\prime}F_{\text{\tiny R}})\Big]\,.

with
\gcd(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})=\gcd(\mathsf{e}^{\prime},\mathsf{m}^{\prime})=1
.
In order to better understand the effect of the resulting topological defect, let us first introduce the integers
\mathsf{n}\equiv\gcd(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{k}\equiv\gcd(\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e}^{\prime})
, so that we can set

\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{n}\,\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\quad,\quad\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{k}\,\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\quad,\quad\mathsf{e}^{\prime}=\mathsf{k}\,\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}\quad,\quad\mathsf{m}^{\prime}=\mathsf{n}\,\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}\,,

(6.66)

with

\displaystyle\gcd(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{k})=\gcd(\mathsf{n},\tilde{\mathsf{m}})=\gcd(\mathsf{k},\tilde{\mathsf{e}})=\gcd(\mathsf{n},\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime})=\gcd(\mathsf{k},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime})=1\,,

(6.67)

\displaystyle\gcd(\tilde{\mathsf{e}},\tilde{\mathsf{m}})=\gcd(\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime})=\gcd(\tilde{\mathsf{e}},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime})=\gcd(\tilde{\mathsf{m}},\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime})=1\,.

Notice that

{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}{\cal S}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{A}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}}&0\\
0&\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}}\end{array}\right)\,,

(6.68)

where
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
{\cal S}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{A}}
are the
{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{Q})
matrices associated to the pairs
(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})
and
(\mathsf{e}^{\prime},\mathsf{m}^{\prime})
as in (
6.28
). Hence, the
{\rm Sp}(2,\mathbb{Q})
transformation
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}{\cal S}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{A}}
corresponds to the minimal defect
{\cal D}^{(\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{A}}
which, indeed, realizes the same gluing conditions of (
6.65
), and hence corresponds to the same GZ transformation
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}{\cal S}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{A}}
. In particular,
{\cal D}^{(\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{A}}
requires that

\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}F_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}F_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}\quad,\quad\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}G_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}G_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}\,.

(6.69)

On the other hand, (
6.65
) imposes stronger constraints on the pull-back of the bulk field strengths along
\Sigma
. Indeed, integrating out
b
and
\tilde{b}
in (
6.65
), we get the conditions:

\displaystyle\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}F_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=\mathsf{k}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}{\rm d}a\quad,\quad\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}F_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}=\mathsf{k}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}{\rm d}a\,.

(6.70)

Given (
6.67
), these conditions admit a solution only if we can set
{\rm d}a=\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}{\rm d}\hat{a}
, for some U(1) gauge field
\hat{a}
on
\Sigma
, and therefore

F_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=\mathsf{k}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}{\rm d}\hat{a}\quad,\quad F_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}=\mathsf{k}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}{\rm d}\hat{a}\,.

(6.71)

Not only do these conditions imply the first of (
6.69
), but they also imply stronger flux quantization conditions along
\Sigma
that, if violated, make the defect vanish.

A similar conclusion holds for the second of (
6.69
). Integrating out
a
and extremizing with respect to the bulk fields
A_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
A_{\text{\tiny R}}
, one gets the conditions:

\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}{\rm d}b=\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}{\rm d}\tilde{b}\quad,\quad G_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=-\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}{\rm d}b\quad,\quad G_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}=-\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}{\rm d}b\,.

(6.72)

The first condition has a solution only if we can set
\mathsf{n}{\rm d}b=\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}{\rm d}\hat{b}
and
\mathsf{n}{\rm d}\tilde{b}=\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}{\rm d}\hat{b}
, for some U(1) gauge field
\hat{b}
. Then, the other two conditions become

G_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=-\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}{\rm d}\hat{b}\quad,\quad G_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}=-\mathsf{n}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime}{\rm d}\hat{b}\,.

(6.73)

These conditions imply the second condition in (
6.69
), but impose stronger quantization conditions along
\Sigma
for
G_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
G_{\text{\tiny R}}
.

The additional flux quantization conditions (
6.71
) and (
6.73
) imposed by the fusion defect (
6.65
) can be associated with the combined action of a condensation defect. More precisely, by matching the corresponding gluing conditions, one can check that
the two defects are related by

{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e}^{\prime},\mathsf{m}^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{A}}={\cal D}^{(\tilde{\mathsf{e}}\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{A}}\times{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{k})}_{\mathsf{A}}\,,

(6.74)

where
{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{k})}_{\mathsf{A}}
is as in (
6.62
), or (
6.63
), and
\hat{a}
in (
6.71
) can be identified with the bulk gauge field living between the two defects on the r.h.s of the equation, before fusing them. For simplicity we have also assumed that
\gcd(\mathsf{k},\tilde{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime})=\gcd(\mathsf{n},\tilde{\mathsf{m}}^{\prime})=1
.
Similar relations hold for more general gcd’s.

6.3
Action of
\tau
-Maxwell non-invertible defects on line operators

In order to read off the action of the GZ defects on line operators we now specialize the discussion in
5
to the
n=1
case. The dyonic line operators can be written as follows,

\displaystyle L_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\exp{\left({\rm i}\tilde{\ell}\oint_{\gamma}\widetilde{A}-{\rm i}\ell\oint_{\gamma}A\right)}\,,

(6.75)

where the integral dyonic charges
\ell,\tilde{\ell}
are identified by the vector
\mathbb{L}=(\tilde{\ell},\ell)^{\rm t}
. If
\gamma=\partial M_{2}
, we can also define the following surface operators,

\widehat{L}_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma,M_{2})=\exp{\left({\rm i}\tilde{\ell}\oint_{M_{2}}G-{\rm i}\ell\oint_{M_{2}}F\right)}\,.

(6.76)

These are well defined also for non-integral charges, and reduce to the genuine line operators (
6.75
) only for integral ones, that is
\widehat{L}_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma,M_{2})=L_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma)
if and only if
{\mathbb{L}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

The gluing conditions (
3.9
) allow us to read off when the line are projected out from the spectrum of genuine line operators or they survive as such. Suppose that
L_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma)
is on the right of
{\cal D}_{\cal S}(\Sigma)
and
\gamma=\partial M_{2}
. Sweeping
{\cal D}_{{\cal S}}(\Sigma)
across
L_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma)
produces the transformation

{\cal D}_{{\cal S}}:\qquad L_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma)\quad~~~\longrightarrow\quad~~~\widehat{L}_{{\cal S}\mathbbm{L}}(M_{2},\gamma)\,,

(6.77)

where
\mathbbm{L}^{\prime}\equiv{\cal S}\mathbbm{L}
corresponds to the new charges

\tilde{\ell}^{\prime}=\mathsf{a}\,\tilde{\ell}+\mathsf{b}\,\ell\quad,\quad\ell^{\prime}=\mathsf{c}\,\tilde{\ell}+\mathsf{d}\,\ell\,.

(6.78)

The new line operators are genuine,
\widehat{L}_{\mathbbm{L}^{\prime}}=L_{\mathbbm{L}^{\prime}}
, if and only if
\tilde{\ell}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
is non-invertible, and hence
{\cal S}
is non-integral, there will always be genuine lines that are mapped to non-genuine ones by
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
as in (
6.77
). On the other hand, an invertible
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
acts by a matrix
{\cal S}\in{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
on electric and magnetic charges and therefore maps genuine lines into genuine lines.

Let us now focus on some specific example. Consider first
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}
. It maps
L_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma)
on the right to
\widehat{L}_{\mathbbm{L}^{\prime}}
on the left, with

\tilde{\ell}^{\prime}=\frac{\sf m}{\sf e}\tilde{\ell}\quad,\quad\ell^{\prime}=\frac{\sf e}{\sf m}\ell\,.

(6.79)

Therefore, when

\frac{\ell\mathsf{e}}{\mathsf{m}}\notin\mathbb{Z}\quad,\quad\frac{\tilde{\ell}\mathsf{m}}{\mathsf{e}}\notin\mathbb{Z}\,,

(6.80)

the defect
\widehat{L}_{\mathbbm{L}^{\prime}}
cannot be considered a genuine line.

The action of
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
on line operators is completely analogous to the action already discussed in
[
122
,
66
]
. In particular
{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}
acts non-invertibly on ’t Hooft lines,
H_{\tilde{\ell}}(\gamma)\equiv L_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma)
with
{\mathbbm{L}=(\tilde{\ell},0)^{\rm t}}
, when
\tilde{\ell}\,\mathsf{p}\neq 0
mod
\mathsf{n}
, and the action reads

{\cal D}^{(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{p})}_{\mathsf{C}}:\quad H_{\tilde{\ell}}(\gamma)\quad~~~\longrightarrow\quad~~~\exp{\left(-{\rm i}\frac{\tilde{\ell}\,\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{n}}\oint_{M_{2}}F\right)}H_{\tilde{\ell}}(\gamma)\,.

(6.81)

If
\tilde{\ell}\,\mathsf{p}=0
mod
\mathsf{n}
, the line remains genuine.

Finally, let us discuss the action of a condensation defect
{\cal C}_{\mathsf{A}}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}
– see (
6.62
) or (
6.63
) – on line operators. Notice that insertion of a line operator (
6.75
) can be seen as a source for
F
and
G
:

{\rm d}F=2\pi\tilde{\ell}\,\delta_{3}(\gamma)\quad,\quad{\rm d}G=2\pi\ell\,\delta_{3}(\gamma)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\rm d}\mathbbm{F}=2\pi\mathbbm{L}\,\delta_{3}(\gamma)\,,

(6.82)

where
\delta_{3}(\gamma)
is a delta-like three-form localized on
\gamma
. Therefore, by using the description (
6.63
)-(
6.64
) of the condensation defects, we get the identity

{\cal C}^{(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m})}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)\;L_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma)={\cal N}_{\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m}}\sum_{C\in H_{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})}\sum_{\tilde{C}\in H_{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{m}})}e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{\mathsf{e}}\ell\,{\rm Link}(C,\gamma)}e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{\ell}\,{\rm Link}(\tilde{C},\gamma)}L_{\mathbbm{L}}(\gamma)

(6.83)

with
{\cal N}^{-1}_{\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m}}={|H^{0}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})||H^{0}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{m}})|}
. In particular, the sum of the phases can give zero, and therefore make the condensation defect act as a projector, as already pointed out in
[
118
,
123
]
. For instance, suppose that
H_{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{e}})
is generated by a single cycle
C_{0}
, with
{\rm Link}(C_{0},\gamma)=1
. Then the sum runs over
C=kC_{0}
with
k=0,\ldots,\mathsf{e}-1
and the right-hand side of (
6.83
) vanishes for
\ell\neq 0
mod
\mathsf{e}
. A similar conclusion holds if
H_{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{m}})
is generated by a single cycle, and also for other values of the linking numbers and/or for other settings.

7
Perturbative Calabi-Yau models

In this section, we apply the general procedure outlined in Section
4
to a class of models with
n
vector fields and GZ symmetry group

\mathscr{G}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\,.

(7.1)

In particular, we will construct concrete examples of GZ topological defects
{\cal D}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\Sigma)
for any
(n-1)
-dimensional rational vector
{\bm{\alpha}}=\{\alpha^{i}\}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq\mathbb{Q}^{n-1}
. We will later comment on the gauging of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, which would reduce the global GZ symmetry group to
{\rm U}(1)^{n-1}
.

These models correspond to the bosonic sector of
{\cal N}=2
supergravities with
n-1
vector multiplets and holomorphic homogeneous prepotential

{\cal F}(X)=-\frac{1}{3!}\frac{\kappa_{ijk}X^{i}X^{j}X^{k}}{X^{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{y}_{IJ}X^{I}X^{J}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\rho(X^{0})^{2}\,.

(7.2)

Here
X^{I}=(X^{0},X^{i})
(
I=0,\ldots,n-1
) are homogeneous complex projective coordinates over the field space parametrized by the
n-1
complex scalar fields

\phi^{i}=\frac{X^{i}}{X^{0}}\,.

(7.3)

In the superconformal formulation,
X^{I}
include a non-physical conformal compensator, which may be identified with
X^{0}
– see for instance
[
128
]
. The constants
\rho
,
\kappa_{ijk}
and
\mathsf{y}_{IJ}
appearing in (
7.2
) are real, and
\kappa_{ijk}
and
\mathsf{y}_{IJ}
are totally symmetric in their indices. For the moment, we just require these constants to define a classically sensible supergravity.
In fact,
\kappa_{ijk}
and
\mathsf{y}_{IJ}
have to satisfy some more stringent constraints, which will be specified presently.

The prepotential (
7.2
) encompasses a large class of models, closely related to the effective theories that one gets in type IIA string compactifications on Calabi-Yau three-folds, neglecting non-perturbative corrections. For convenience, we will loosely speaking refer to these models as perturbative Calabi-Yau models, although our arguments will be independent of this possible realization. In fact, as in the rest of the paper, we will continue to adopt an agnostic approach regarding the UV completion.

Ignoring the gravitational sector, the bosonic sector of these models can be identified as a subclass of the models of Appendix
A
, with
n
U(1) vectors
A^{I}
, and the
n-1
complex scalar fields
\phi^{i}
, which we split in real and imaginary parts as follows

\phi^{i}\equiv a^{i}+{\rm i}s^{i}\,.

(7.4)

As will become clear shortly, we can refer to
a^{i}
and
s^{i}
and axions and saxions, respectively. Their kinetic terms take the form

-\mathsf{f}^{2}K_{i\bar{\jmath}}\,{\rm d}\phi^{i}\wedge*{\rm d}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{\jmath}}\,,\quad~~~~~\text{with}\quad K_{i\bar{\jmath}}=\frac{\partial^{2}K}{\partial\phi^{i}\partial\bar{\phi}^{\bar{\jmath}}}\ ,

(7.5)

where
\mathsf{f}
identifies a reference mass-scale (which in supergravity coincides with the Planck mass) and the metric is associated with the Kähler potential

K(\phi,\bar{\phi})=-\log\left[P(s)-\frac{1}{4}\rho\right]\,,\quad~~~~~\text{with}\quad P(s)\equiv\frac{1}{3!}\kappa_{ijk}s^{i}s^{j}s^{k}\,.

(7.6)

For us, the important point is that the Kähler potential, and hence the kinetic terms (
7.5
), are invariant under arbitrary axionic shifts
a^{i}\rightarrow a^{i}+\alpha^{i}
,
\alpha^{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, namely

\phi^{i}\rightarrow\phi^{i}+\alpha^{i}\,.

(7.7)

For the moment we treat
a^{i}
as non-periodic, postponing the periodicity requirements to a later step. Hence, adopting the terminology introduced in Section
2
, we can identify the symmetry group of the neutral sector with (
7.1
).

We now turn to the gauge sector. The kinetic matrix of the gauge fields takes the form (
A.1
) with

{\cal N}_{IJ}\equiv\overline{{\cal F}}_{IJ}+2{\rm i}\,\frac{(NX)_{I}(NX)_{J}}{X^{T}NX}\,.

(7.8)

where
{\cal F}_{IJ}\equiv\partial^{2}{\cal F}(X)/\partial X^{I}\partial X^{J}
and
N_{IJ}\equiv{\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}{\cal F}_{IJ}
. By computing the explicit form of
{\cal N}_{IJ}
associated with (
7.2
) one finds that it has a non-trivial, up to cubic, dependence on the axions
a^{i}
, which breaks the invariance of the Lagrangian under the axionic shifts (
7.7
).
20
20

20

See
[
129
]
for a detailed discussion of the breaking of invertible symmetries induced by these generalized theta-terms, and
[
130
]
for an earlier proposal to resurrect some of the axionic shift symmetries (
7.7
) as non-invertible ones.

We will not need the detailed form of such dependence, but rather the transformation that it induces on
{\cal N}_{IJ}
under (
7.7
). This takes the form

{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi+\alpha)=\big(\big[\mathsf{C}(\alpha)+\mathsf{D}(\alpha){\cal N}(\phi)\big]\big[\mathsf{A}(\alpha)+\mathsf{B}(\alpha){\cal N}(\phi)\big]^{-1}\big)_{IJ}\,,

(7.9)

where the
n\times n
matrices
\mathsf{A,B,C,D}
, which identify a symplectic matrix
{\cal S}
according to (
2.23
), are specified by the following block decomposition,

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}^{I}{}_{J}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{A}^{0}{}_{0}&\mathsf{A}^{0}{}_{j}\\
\mathsf{A}^{i}{}_{0}&\mathsf{A}^{i}{}_{j}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\alpha^{i}&\delta^{i}{}_{j}\end{array}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}^{IJ}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(7.10e)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{IJ}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{C}_{00}&\mathsf{C}_{0j}\\
\mathsf{C}_{i0}&\mathsf{C}_{ij}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P(\alpha)+2\mathsf{y}_{0k}\alpha^{k}&P_{j}(\alpha)+\mathsf{y}_{jk}\alpha^{k}\\
\mathsf{y}_{ik}\alpha^{k}-P_{i}(\alpha)&-P_{ij}(\alpha)\end{array}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{I}{}^{J}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{A}^{-1})^{J}{}_{I}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}1&-\alpha^{j}\\
0&\delta_{i}{}^{j}\end{array}\right)\,,

with
P_{i}(\alpha)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{ijk}\alpha^{j}\alpha^{k}
and
P_{ij}(\alpha)\equiv\kappa_{ijk}\alpha^{k}
.
Through (
2.23
), the matrices (
7.10
) define a symplectic representation

\mathscr{G}\ni\{\alpha^{i}\}\ \mapsto\ {\cal S}(\alpha)\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

(7.11)

of the GZ symmetry group (
7.1
). As in the rest of the paper, we will simply identify
\mathscr{G}
with its image in
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
under this map. Note that (
7.9
) takes the form (
A.8
), and therefore provides the realization of the GZ condition (
2.14
).

7.1
GZ topological defects

The parameters
\alpha^{i}
identify a basis of generators
T_{i}\in{\rm sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
of the symplectic representation of the abelian group (
7.1
), so that (
3.2
) is realized with
T=\alpha^{i}T_{i}
, namely,
{\cal S}(\alpha)=\exp\left(\alpha^{i}T_{i}\right)
. We can then write the defect
{\cal U}_{{\alpha}}(\Sigma)
implementing the axionic shift (
7.7
) as in (
3.4
), by choosing
{\cal J}_{T}=\alpha^{i}{\cal J}_{i}
with

{\cal J}_{i}=-\mathsf{f}^{2}K_{ij}(s)*{\rm d}a^{j}\,.

(7.12)

As in our general discussion, the currents
{\cal J}_{i}
are not conserved, and hence
{\cal U}_{\alpha}(\Sigma)
is not topological, since

{\rm d}{\cal J}_{i}=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\kappa_{ijk}F^{j}\wedge F^{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi}F^{0}\wedge G_{i}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{y}_{0i}F^{0}\wedge F^{0}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{y}_{ij}F^{0}\wedge F^{j}\,.

(7.13)

This codifies the breaking of the axionic shift symmetries (
7.7
) due to the non-invariance (
7.9
) of the kinetric matrices (
7.8
) of the gauge fields.

In order to construct GZ topological defects, we must
impose some further conditions on the constants
\kappa_{ijk}
and
\mathsf{y}_{ij}
. More precisely, we require that the integral shifts
\alpha^{i}=m^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
identify the integral subgroup
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
. By consistency with our general construction, the matrices (
7.10
) must take integral values and hence define elements
{\cal S}({m})
of
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z})
. (Again, we will for simplicity use the same symbol for
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
as well as for its representation in
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z})
). It is easy to see that this is possible if and only if

\displaystyle\kappa_{ijk}\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(7.14a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{ijk}m^{j}m^{k}-\mathsf{y}_{ij}m^{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(7.14b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\kappa_{ijk}m^{i}m^{j}m^{k}+2\mathsf{y}_{0i}m^{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(7.14c)

for any choice of integers
m^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, these imply that

\mathsf{y}_{ij}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\quad,\quad\mathsf{y}_{0i}\in\frac{1}{12}\mathbb{Z}\,.

(7.15)

The conditions (
7.14
) are indeed realized in type IIA Calabi-Yau compactifications. In that case, the condition (
7.14a
) is satisfied since the constants
\kappa_{ijk}
can be identified with triple intersection numbers of the Calabi-Yau space.
21
21

21

Furthermore, the conditions (
7.14b
) and (
7.14c
) are a consequence of the
stronger
conditions derived in
[
131
]
. More precisely, (
7.14b
) is equivalent to the first of
[
131
, Eq.(4.12)]
, while (
7.14c
) is implied by the second of
[
131
, Eq.(4.12)]
, since
P(q)+\frac{1}{12}c_{2,i}q^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note also that, in our agnostic approach, the real constant
\rho
appearing in (
7.2
) is arbitrary, while in type IIA realizations it takes the fixed value
\rho=\chi\,\zeta(3)/(2\pi)^{3}
, where
\chi
is the Euler number of the Calabi-Yau three-fold
[
132
]
.

Now, according to the general discussion of Sections
3
and
4
, we can construct GZ topological defects for elements of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathscr{G}\cap{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Q})
. In our setting,
{\cal S}({\alpha})\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
if

\alpha^{i}=\alpha^{i}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\equiv\frac{q^{i}}{p^{i}}\in\mathbb{Q}\,,

(7.16)

with
q^{i},p^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. We may also impose
\gcd(q^{i},p^{i})=1
for any
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, but most of the following results hold also for more general choices.
The corresponding GZ topological defect can be factorized as in (
3.1
), with
{\cal U}
defect

{\cal U}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}(\Sigma)\equiv\exp\left({\rm i}\sum_{i}\frac{q^{i}}{p^{i}}\oint_{\Sigma}{\cal J}_{i}\right)\,.

(7.17)

It hence remains to identify a realization of the interfaces
{\cal W}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}(\Sigma)\equiv{\cal W}_{{\cal S}(\alpha_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})}(\Sigma)
.

As a first step, we need to factorize the symplectic matrix
{\cal S}(\alpha)
defined by (
7.10
) as a product of matrices (
2.24
). As a simple possibility, we can write it as

{\cal S}({\alpha})={\cal S}_{\tilde{\mathsf{C}}({\alpha})}{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}({\alpha})}\,,

(7.18)

with
{\sf A}(\alpha)
is as in (
7.10
) and

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{C}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathsf{C}\mathsf{A}^{-1})(\alpha)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{00}&\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{0j}\\
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{i0}&\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{ij}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-2P(\alpha)+2\mathsf{y}_{0k}\alpha^{k}-\mathsf{y}_{kl}\alpha^{k}\alpha^{l}&P_{j}(\alpha)+\mathsf{y}_{jk}\alpha^{k}\\
P_{i}(\alpha)+\mathsf{y}_{ik}\alpha^{k}&-P_{ij}(\alpha)\end{array}\right)\,,

(7.19)

where we have used (
7.10
). We can then get a realization of
{\cal W}_{{\cal S}(\alpha)}(\Sigma)
by concatenating
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{C}
interfaces according to the decomposition (
7.18
).

We first focus on the
A
defect
{\cal W}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)\equiv{\cal W}_{\mathsf{A}(\alpha_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})}(\Sigma)
. Following the construction outlined in Section
4.2
we need to identify a factorization of the form (
4.2
). For instance, a simple choice is

\displaystyle(\mathsf{E}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{E}^{0}{}_{0}&\mathsf{E}^{0}{}_{j}\\
\mathsf{E}^{i}{}_{0}&\mathsf{E}^{i}{}_{j}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&p^{i}\delta^{i}{}_{j}\end{array}\right)\,,

(7.20)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{M}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{M}^{0}{}_{0}&\mathsf{M}^{0}{}_{j}\\
\mathsf{M}^{i}{}_{0}&\mathsf{M}^{i}{}_{j}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
q^{i}&p^{i}\delta^{i}{}_{j}\end{array}\right)\,.

Using this or any other decomposition in (
4.3
), we get

{\cal W}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)=\int{\cal D}b\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge\left[(\mathsf{E}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}-(\mathsf{M}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]\right)\,.

(7.21)

In general, the factorization defined by (
7.20
) is not left-coprime, and then (
7.21
) is not minimal in the sense discussed in Section
4.2
. However, its simplicity allows us to easily combine it with other interfaces and get a readable explicit general formula.

Let us now turn to the
{\cal S}_{\tilde{\mathsf{C}}(\alpha_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})}
element appearing in (
7.18
). Taking the conditions (
7.14
) and (
7.15
) into account, from (
7.19
) it is clear that
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}_{IJ}\equiv\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{IJ}(\alpha_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})\in\mathbb{Q}
. Again, we must pick an integral factorization of the form (
4.25
) and, as discussed in Section
4.3
, the canonical choice would correspond to a right-coprime factorization. This would allow us to use a minimal TQFT and to
construct the corresponding minimal
\mathsf{C}
interface. However, as in the construction of (
7.21
), for our purposes it is convenient to consider a more general factorization and use it in (
4.38
).

So, in order to realize (
4.25
) for any choice of
q^{i}
and
p^{i}
, we must find an invertible matrix
(\mathsf{N}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}
such that

(\mathsf{P}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}})_{IJ}\equiv\tilde{\mathsf{C}}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}_{IK}\,(\mathsf{N}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{K}{}_{J}\in\mathbb{Z}\,,\quad\forall I,J=0,\ldots,n-1.

(7.22)

The simplest possibility is obtained by picking a positive integer
N_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}
such that
N_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\tilde{\mathsf{C}}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}_{KL}\in\mathbb{Z}
for any
K,L=0,\ldots,n-1
, and setting

(\mathsf{N}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}=N_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\delta^{I}{}_{J}\,.

(7.23)

We can now use (
4.38
) in (
4.23
) to construct a (non-minimal)
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}
interface
{\cal W}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{C}}}(\Sigma)
.
By concatenating it with (
7.21
) according to (
7.18
), we get the complete
{\cal W}
interface:

{\cal W}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}={\cal W}_{\tilde{\mathsf{C}}}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\times{\cal W}_{\mathsf{A}}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\,.

(7.24)

Combining it with (
7.17
) as in (
3.1
), we then arrive at the GZ topological defects

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}(\Sigma)=

\displaystyle\exp\left({\rm i}\,\alpha^{i}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\oint_{\Sigma}{\cal J}_{i}\right)\int{\cal D}b\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge\left[(\mathsf{M}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}-(\mathsf{E}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]\right)

(7.25)

\displaystyle\int{\cal D}a{\cal D}c\,\exp\left(-\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\mathsf{K}_{IJ}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\oint_{\Sigma}a^{I}\wedge f^{J}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}c_{I}\wedge\left[(\tilde{\mathsf{N}}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}f^{J}-F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\right]\right)\,,

where
\mathsf{K}_{IJ}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\equiv(\mathsf{N}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{K}{}_{I}(\mathsf{N}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{L}{}_{J}\tilde{\mathsf{C}}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}_{KL}
. To make (
7.25
) more concrete, we can pick
\mathsf{M}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}
as in (
7.20
) and
\mathsf{N}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}
as in (
7.23
), so that
\mathsf{K}_{IJ}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}=N^{2}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\tilde{\mathsf{C}}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}_{IJ}
.
This provides just one possible realization of GZ defects implementing rational axionic shifts. Many other examples could of course be constructed and,
in particular, it would be interesting to identify and study the minimal TQFT dressing these interfaces.

Note that for integral axionic shifts,
\alpha^{i}=m^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can just pick
q^{i}=m^{i}
and
p^{i}=1
and choose
(\mathsf{M}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}=\delta^{I}{}_{J}
and
(\mathsf{E}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{I}{}_{J}=(\mathsf{A}_{{\bf m}})^{I}{}_{J}\equiv\mathsf{A}^{I}{}_{J}(m)
. Furthermore the conditions (
7.14
) ensures that
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}^{\bf m}_{IJ}\equiv\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{IJ}(m)\in\mathbb{Z}
for any
m^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that we can choose
(\mathsf{N}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}})^{L}{}_{J}=\delta^{I}{}_{J}
and
\mathsf{K}_{IJ}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}=\mathsf{P}_{IJ}^{{\bf q},{\bf p}}=\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{IJ}^{\bf m}
. In this case, one can explicitly integrate out
c_{I}
and set
f^{I}=F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
, getting the invertible topological defect

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{{\bf m}}(\Sigma)=

\displaystyle\exp\left({\rm i}\,m^{i}\oint_{\Sigma}{\cal J}_{i}-\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\tilde{\mathsf{C}}^{\bf m}_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}\right)

(7.26)

\displaystyle\times\int{\cal D}b\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge\left[F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}-(\mathsf{A}_{{\bf m}})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right]\right)\,.

This coincides with the defect that can be more directly obtained by combining the invertible
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{C}
interfaces appearing in (
4.1
).

It would be important to better understand the spectrum of possible GZ topological defects in this class of models, and their fusion rules.
This is particularly crucial if one wants to consider periodic axions, that is, to impose the identification
\phi^{i}\simeq\phi^{i}+m^{i}
,
m^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, differently from what assumed so far. This periodicity would amount to gauge the integral axionic shifts, hence summing over all possible insertions of the invertible defects (
7.26
) in the path-integral. Since the GZ group (
7.1
) is abelian, the adjoint action of the
{\cal D}_{\bf m}
defects relates non-invertible GZ topological defects
{\cal D}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}
implementing the same gluing conditions which, however, do not in general coincide. Only linear combinations of GZ topological defects that are invariant under this adjoint action for any
m^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
correspond to global symmetries of the theory with periodic axions. We will return to these aspects in Section
8
from a more general point of view, leaving a more detailed study of this class of models to future work.

7.2
A one-modulus example

Just to get an idea of what the above formulas can more concretely look like, we consider a simple example, related to the compactification of type IIA theory on the quintic Calabi-Yau. Namely, we focus on its low-energy bosonic sector, corresponding to one complex field
\phi
and two gauge fields,
A^{0}
and
A^{1}
. In this case
the exact
perturbative
prepotential takes the form (
7.2
) with
X^{I}=(X^{0},X^{1})
,
\kappa_{111}=5
and
[
132
]

\mathsf{y}_{00}=0\quad,\quad\mathsf{y}_{01}=\frac{1}{12}\quad,\quad\mathsf{y}_{11}=\frac{1}{2}\,,

(7.27)

up to possible additional integral integral contributions to
\mathsf{y}_{IJ}
which do not change the physics. (One can easily check that the conditions (
7.14
) are satisfied.)
There is only one complex field
\phi=a+{\rm i}s=X^{1}/X^{0}
. As in the rest of this section, we assume an agnostic viewpoint, taking
a\in\mathbb{R}
and ignoring the fact that in string models
a
is actually periodic. The Kähler potential (
7.6
) becomes, up to an irrelevant additive constant:

K=-\log\left(s^{3}-\frac{3}{10}\rho\right)\,.

(7.28)

It is interesting to observe that a non-vanishing
\rho
breaks the SL
(2,\mathbb{R})
symmetry of the scalar metric associated with
\rho=0
. If
\rho\neq 0
, the only surviving Killing direction is the axionic shift
a\rightarrow a+\alpha
. (In the type IIA string theory model,
\rho
has the specific value
25\zeta(3)/\pi^{3}
and represents a perturbative loop correction
[
132
]
.)

Let us consider rational axionic shifts
\alpha=\alpha_{\bf{q,p}}=q/p
,
q,p\in\mathbb{Z}
,
p\geq 1
. The transformation is determined by the matrices

\mathsf{A}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\frac{q}{p}&1\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}=\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\frac{5q^{3}}{3p^{3}}-\frac{q^{2}}{2p^{2}}+\frac{q}{6p}&\frac{5q^{2}}{2p^{2}}+\frac{q}{2p}\\
\frac{5q^{2}}{2p^{2}}+\frac{q}{2p}&-\frac{5q}{p}\end{array}\right)\,.

(7.29)

We may then choose the factorization
\mathsf{A}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}=\mathsf{E}^{-1}_{\bf{q,p}}\mathsf{M}_{\bf{q,p}}
with

\mathsf{E}_{\bf{q,p}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&p\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad\mathsf{M}_{\bf{q,p}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
q&p\end{array}\right)\,,

(7.30)

as in (
7.20
), and
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}=\mathsf{P}_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}\mathsf{N}_{\bf{q,p}}^{-1}
, where
(\mathsf{N}_{\bf{q,p}})^{I}{}_{J}=N_{\bf q,\bf p}\delta^{I}{}_{J}
as in (
7.23
) with

N_{{\bf q},{\bf p}}=6p^{3}\,.

(7.31)

This satisfies (
7.22
) for any choice of
q
and
p
. Consequently
\mathsf{K}^{\bf{q,p}}
as defined after (
7.25
) becomes

\mathsf{K}^{\bf{q,p}}=6p^{3}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-10q^{3}+3q^{2}p-qp^{2}&15q^{2}p+3qp^{2}\\
15q^{2}p+3qp^{2}&-30qp^{2}\end{array}\right)

(7.32)

Given these quantities, one can easily write down the corresponding GZ topological defect (
7.25
).

8
Remarks on gauging and symmetry breaking

So far we have been completely agnostic about the UV completion of the GZ models. As emphasized in the Introduction, GZ models should generically be interpreted as effective field theories, valid only at low-energy. In a rigid QFT context, the GZ global symmetries constructed in this paper may or may not admit a counterpart in the UV complete theory. On the other hand, once quantum gravity is taken into account, the GZ symmetries are expected to be either gauged or approximate at low-energy, as any other global (invertible or non-invertible) symmetry
[
82
,
83
,
84
]
.

Various combined mechanisms could be responsible for the realization of this expectation, already at low energies. For instance, (part of) the non-invertible
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
symmetries may have a mixed ’t Hooft anomaly with gravity, of the kind described for instance in
[
106
]
, which might automatically break these symmetries once we couple a dynamical gravitational sector. While we postpone a detailed discussion of this possibility to the future, in this section we would like to briefly describe a simple alternative (and/or complementary) symmetry breaking mechanism, that would work even without coupling a dynamical gravitational sector. Suppose that the symplectic representation (
2.11
) of
\mathscr{G}
is faithful, so that
\mathscr{G}
can be regarded as a subgroup of
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, and let us consider what happens if we try to gauge the subgroup
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
of invertible GZ symmetries (as suggested by experience with string theory models), assuming that it has no pure ’t Hooft anomalies. In terms of the corresponding GZ defects
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
, constructed by combining the interfaces
(
4.1
) and the invertible defects (
3.4
), this gauging corresponds to summing over all possible
insertions of the defects
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
with
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}

[
1
]
.

Consider now a
non
-integral element
{\cal R}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and a corresponding non-invertible GZ defect
{\cal D}_{\cal R}
. For any
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we then have

{\cal D}^{-1}_{\cal S}\times{\cal D}_{\cal R}\times{\cal D}_{\cal S}={\cal D}_{{\cal S}^{-1}{\cal R}{\cal S}}\,,

(8.1)

where
{\cal D}_{{\cal S}^{-1}{\cal R}{\cal S}}
is a GZ defect associated with the non-integral
{\cal S}^{-1}{\cal R}{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. It is now clear that, if
{\cal S}^{-1}{\cal R}{\cal S}\neq{\cal R}
, then
{\cal D}_{\cal R}
is not a well defined operator in the
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
gauged theory. In this sense, the non-invertible symmetry associated with
{\cal D}_{\cal R}
is broken. Notice that this breaking is directly correlated with the breaking of the magnetic and electric U(1) one-form symmetries, whose two-form currents
F^{I}
and
G_{I}
transform as in (
2.9b
) and are, at least partially, removed from the spectrum of gauge invariant operators. This correlation follows from the realization of the GZ defects in terms of the half-space gaugings described in Appendices
D
and
E
.

The above duality-induced symmetry breaking mechanism may fail if
\mathscr{G}
is abelian, or, more generically, if
{\cal R}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is invariant under
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
conjugation. The simplest example is provided by the axion-Mawell model discussed in Section
6.1
, in which
{\cal D}_{\cal S}\times{\cal D}_{{\cal R}}={\cal D}_{{\cal R}}\times{\cal D}_{\cal S}
, for any
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
and any
{\cal R}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq\mathbb{Q}
. One can then gauge
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e. assume that the axion is periodic as in
[
66
,
23
,
122
]
, without breaking the GZ non-invertible symmetries. However, this conclusion does not a priori apply to any abelian
\mathscr{G}
. Indeed, in general we have

{\cal D}^{-1}_{\cal S}\times{\cal D}_{\cal R}\times{\cal D}_{\cal S}={\cal D}^{\prime}_{{\cal R}}\,,

(8.2)

where, for each
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
,
{\cal D}^{\prime}_{{\cal R}}
may be different from the
{\cal D}_{\cal R}
. This possibility is due to the degeneracy of non-invertible GZ defects associated with the same element of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

The survival of the non-invertible symmetry in the
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
-gauged theory appears strictly related to the existence of a unique or of a finite number of “minimal” topological GZ defects in the parent ungauged theory. Indeed, we expect that the
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
action (
8.2
) preserves the defect minimality. So, if
an element
{\cal R}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
corresponds to a finite number of minimal GZ defects of the ungauged theory,
finite linear combination thereof may give a gauge invariant topological defect, which would hence survive in the
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
gauged theory. For instance, this is what we expect to happen in the class of models studied in Section
7
. Clearly, properly testing these qualitative expectations would require a better understanding of the minimal defects, which possibly requires a case-by-case study and is beyond the scope of this paper.

9
Conclusions

In this paper we have shown that the classical symmetries described by Gaillard-Zumino in
[
64
]
, which involve continuous electromagnetic duality rotations, admit a quantum realization in terms of non-invertible operators. Our conclusion applies to any non-gravitational GZ model and any GZ symmetry group
\mathscr{G}
. In absence of mixed ’t Hooft anomalies between the GZ symmetries and gravity, this conclusion can be immediately extended to gravitational GZ models as well. More precisely, we have shown that for any rational GZ symmetry
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\mathscr{G}
one can construct a corresponding topological defect
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
. This defect is non-invertible, unless
{\cal S}
belongs to the subgroup
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
of integral GZ symmetries. Our general claims have been illustrated in some concrete GZ models. Other well-known examples of GZ models are provided by theories with extended supersymmetry, such as the maximal ungauged supergravity
[
67
,
68
]
. However, we emphasize that our results are completely independent of supersymmetry.

Starting from the general results presented in this paper one may proceed in various possible directions.

•

First of all, we have provided just a ‘proof of principle’ of the existence of non-invertible GZ symmetries, emphasizing that the degeneracy of non-invertible GZ defects. Namely, the fact that each GZ transformation
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
corresponds to several, and potentially infinite, non-invertible defects
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
. On the other hand, for specific transformations, namely
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{C}
transformations, we showed that there exist ‘minimal’ defects
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
, which then play a distinguished role and reduce the degeneration.
It would be extremely interesting to better understand whether this is true more generically, namely if there is some notion of minimality which holds for more general defects and which implies that, for any given
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, there exists a finite number of minimal defects
{\cal D}_{\cal S}
, or maybe even a unique one.

•

We have already emphasized the relevance of the electric and magnetic one-form symmetries in the construction of the GZ defects. It would be interesting to more systematically investigate the interplay of our GZ non-invertible (zero-form) symmetries with other higher-form symmetries, as done for QED
[
66
]
and for axion-Maxwell
[
122
]
, and to understand their overall categorical structure
[
56
,
125
]
. Finally, it would be interesting to analyze the fate of GZ-symmetries once charged fermions are added.

•

An interface
{\cal W}_{\cal S}
becomes a topological defect for theories with a decoupled neutral sector at a fixed point of the
{\cal S}
action. This observation is particularly interesting when the neutral sector includes a moduli space of scalar fields. At these fixed points, only a (discrete) subgroup of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
survives. For instance, in the
n=1
case the symmetries surviving at
{\cal S}
fixed points should correspond to the non-invertible defects studied in
[
55
,
117
,
123
,
133
]
. It would be interesting to understand this mechanism in more detail, and to clarify the physical interplay between the choice of a vacuum in the moduli space and the
{\cal W}_{\cal S}
interfaces that survive as symmetry defects at the
{\cal S}
fixed point.
A related question is to what extent the spontaneous breaking of the GZ non-invertible symmetries captures the structure of the neutral sector away from the fixed points, in the spirit of
[
63
,
134
]
, or alternatively as in
[
135
,
136
]
.
Moreover, as studied in
[
137
,
42
]
, non-Lagrangian
\mathcal{N}=2
four-dimensional class S theories exhibit non-invertible symmetries forming subgroups of the symplectic group
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Q})
, and it is natural to ask whether they admit any relation with the non-invertible GZ symmetries studied in this paper.

•

It is crucial to study the anomalies of the GZ symmetries we have constructed. Such anomalies would provide obstructions to gauging subgroups of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and may be relevant in the context of gauged supergravity theories. Even more interestingly, there may exist mixed anomalies with the gravitational sector, of the type discussed in
[
106
]
. If not curable by some cancellation mechanism, for instance as the one at work on D3-branes
[
138
]
or along the lines of
[
108
]
, these anomalies would provide a purely field-theoretic gravitational symmetry breaking mechanism of these non-invertible symmetries.
Anomalies of non-invertible symmetries are, however, more intricate to formulate than in the standard invertible case. Recent progress in this direction has been made in
[
139
,
140
,
141
]
. A particularly powerful approach is the symmetry topological field theory (SymTFT) framework introduced in
[
142
,
143
,
144
,
145
,
146
]
, which has recently been generalized to include non-abelian continuous symmetries
[
147
,
148
,
149
,
150
]
. The SymTFT framework provides a systematic way to analyze non-invertible defects and their anomalies, and therefore may serve as a useful alternative viewpoint on our construction of GZ non-invertible symmetries.

•

In Section
8
we outlined a simple mechanism that leads, in one shot, to the gauging of GZ invertible symmetries and the breaking of GZ non-invertible ones. In combination with the possible mixed gravitational anomalies mentioned above, this may further contribute to
the expected absence of global symmetries in quantum gravity models, without needing the explicit incorporation of UV ingredients. This
mechanism may provide a purely bottom-up motivation for the gauging of the
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
subgroup. Of course, the possibility of realizing this mechanism depends on the potential presence of (pure) ’t Hooft anomalies for
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, of the kind e.g. studied in
[
110
]
, which would obstruct the gauging of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. In addition, the efficiency of this mechanism depends on the structure of the theory and of its GZ group
\mathscr{G}
. It would be extremely interesting to explore the connection of this observation with the alternative bottom-up arguments for the existence of U-duality groups recently given in
[
100
]
, and with the related results of
[
101
]
, which focuses on models with symmetric moduli spaces and study in detail their asymptotic limits.
On the other hand, a world-sheet realization of our symmetries, along the lines of
[
74
]
, would provide a useful top-down perspective thereon, which may also clarify a potential connection with the results of
[
151
,
95
]
.

Appendix A
Two-derivative models

In this Appendix we review the realization of the GZ conditions described in Section
2
in two-derivative models including only
n
vectors
A^{I}
and a set of neutral massless scalars
\phi^{i}
parametrizing a moduli space
{\cal M}
.
Important examples of these GZ models are provided by coset models, which are discussed in some detail in
[
64
]
and for instance appear in extended supergravities.

The most general two-derivative Lagrangian for such a field content takes the form

\displaystyle{\cal L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}{\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)F^{I}\wedge*F^{J}+\frac{1}{4\pi}{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)F^{I}\wedge F^{J}-\frac{1}{2}{\cal G}_{ij}(\phi){\rm d}\phi^{i}\wedge*{\rm d}\phi^{j}

(A.1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\left[{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)F^{I}_{+}\wedge F^{J}_{+}\right]-\frac{1}{2}{\cal G}_{ij}(\phi){\rm d}\phi^{i}\wedge*{\rm d}\phi^{j}\,,

where we have grouped the field dependent gauge couplings and theta terms in the complex symmetric matrix
{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)={\cal N}_{JI}(\phi)
, and we have introduced the imaginary self-dual field strengths

F_{+}^{I}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(F^{I}-{\rm i}*F^{I})\quad~~~~\Rightarrow\quad~~~~*F_{+}^{I}={\rm i}F^{I}\,.

(A.2)

The
\tau
-Maxwell theory of Section
6.2
is a particular case with
n=1
and
{\cal N}=-\tau
.

The dual field strengths
G_{I}
as defined in (
2.3
) are given by

G_{I}={\rm Im\hskip 1.00006pt}{\cal N}_{IJ}*F^{J}+{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}{\cal N}_{IJ}F^{J}\,.

(A.3)

It may also be useful to rewrite this equation in terms of the imaginary self-dual combinations
G^{+}_{I}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(G_{I}-{\rm i}*G_{I})
:

G^{+}_{I}={\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)F^{J}_{+}\,.

(A.4)

The Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{\cal S}
appearing in (
2.13
) is more easily obtained by imposing that the form of (
A.4
) is preserved under a general symplectic transformation (
2.9b
). Namely, we should have
\tilde{G}^{+}_{I}=\tilde{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)\tilde{F}^{J}_{+}
for some
\tilde{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)
. Decomposing
{\cal S}
as in (
2.23
) and imposing (
2.9b
) we get the identity

\tilde{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)=\big(\big[\mathsf{C}+\mathsf{D}{\cal N}(\phi)\big]\big[\mathsf{A}+\mathsf{B}{\cal N}(\phi)\big]^{-1}\big)_{IJ}\,.

(A.5)

The Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{\cal S}(\tilde{F},\phi)
is then obtained by replacing
{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)
and
F^{I}
with
\tilde{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)
and
\tilde{F}
, respectively, in (
A.1
). Notice that the condition (
2.13
) is trivially satisfied, because of

{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\left[{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)F^{I}_{+}\wedge F^{J}_{+}\right]={\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\left(F^{I}_{+}\wedge G_{I}^{+}\right)=\frac{1}{2}F^{I}\wedge G_{I}\,,

(A.6)

and of the analogous identity with tilded quantities. Hence

{\cal L}(F,\phi)-\frac{1}{4\pi}F^{I}\wedge G_{I}={\cal L}_{\cal S}(\tilde{F},\phi)-\frac{1}{4\pi}\tilde{F}^{I}\wedge\tilde{G}_{I}=-\frac{1}{2}{\cal G}_{ij}(\phi){\rm d}\phi^{i}\wedge*{\rm d}\phi^{j}\,.

(A.7)

So far, we have not imposed any non-trivial GZ restriction yet. Imposing (
2.14
) we conclude that, if
{\cal S}\in\mathscr{G}
,
f_{\cal S}(\phi)
must define an isometry of the moduli space metric
{\rm d}s^{2}_{\cal M}\equiv{\cal G}_{ij}(\phi){\rm d}\phi^{i}{\rm d}\phi^{j}
and
{\cal N}_{IJ}
must transform as follows:

{\cal N}_{IJ}(f_{\cal S}(\phi))=\tilde{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)\,.

(A.8)

For instance, in the
\tau
-Maxwell theory this condition corresponds to (
6.23
).

In terms of the infinitesimal deformations (
2.2
),
\xi_{T}(\phi)
is a Killing vector of
{\rm d}s^{2}_{\cal M}
. It acts on
{\cal N}_{IJ}
as follows

\delta_{T}{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)=\xi_{T}^{i}(\phi)\partial_{i}{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)=\left(W+Y{\cal N}-{\cal N}U-{\cal N}V{\cal N}\right)_{IJ}\,,

(A.9)

where we used the decomposition (
1.1
) of
T
. The corresponding current defined by (
2.20
) is

{\cal J}_{T}=-g_{ij}(\phi)\xi^{i}(\phi)*{\rm d}\phi^{j}\,.

(A.10)

By using the
\phi^{i}
equations of motion

E_{i}={\cal G}_{ij}D*{\rm d}\phi^{j}+\frac{1}{2\pi}{\rm Re\hskip 1.00006pt}\left[\partial_{i}{\cal N}_{IJ}(\phi)F^{I}_{+}\wedge F^{J}_{+}\right]=0\,,

(A.11)

with
D*{\rm d}\phi^{i}\equiv{\rm d}*{\rm d}\phi^{i}+\Gamma^{i}{}_{jk}(\phi){\rm d}\phi^{j}\wedge*{\rm d}\phi^{j}
, one can then check the general identity (
1.2
).

Appendix B
Integral symplectic transformations and interfaces

In this Appendix we review how the integral symplectic group
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z})
acts on GZ Lagrangians and we construct the corresponding interfaces (
4.1
), following
[
112
,
113
,
114
]
.
The general element of
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z})
can be decomposed as the product of integral powers of (
2.24
), with
\mathsf{A}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{C}^{\rm t}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Z})

[
105
]
. Thus, we can focus on these three cases, as more general
{\cal W}_{\cal S}
interfaces with
{\cal S}\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z})
can be obtained by concatenating these
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{C}
and
\Omega
interfaces and their inverses. By adopting the same notation introduced in Section
2.2
, we will denote the corresponding transformed Lagrangians as
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}}\equiv{\cal L}_{{\cal S}_{\mathsf{A}}}
,
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{C}}\equiv{\cal L}_{{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}}
and
{\cal L}_{\Omega}\equiv{\cal L}_{{\cal S}_{\Omega}}
, respectively.

B.1
A
transformations and interfaces

An
{\sf A}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
transformation amounts to just the reshuffle of the U(1) gauge fields

\tilde{F}^{I}=\mathsf{A}^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}\,.

(B.1)

The new Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}}
is then simply defined by
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}}(\tilde{F},\phi)={\cal L}(F,\phi)
and is clearly physically equivalent to
{\cal L}(F,\phi)
, provided the gauge fields are related as in (
B.1
).

The
{\cal W}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)
interface, connecting two such equivalent descriptions as in (
3.7
), must only implement the correct gluing conditions. It is easy to see that this requirement is realized by the insertion of the interface

{\cal W}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)=\int{\cal D}b\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge(F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}-\mathsf{A}^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}})\right]\,,

(B.2)

where
b_{I}
are U(1) world-volume connections on
W
. Indeed, integrating out
b_{I}
one gets the gluing conditions
F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=\mathsf{A}^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
. On the other hand, the infinitesimal variation of the gauge fields
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}
produces a term localized on
\Sigma
, which vanishes only if
G_{{\text{\tiny L}}\,I}|_{\Sigma}=-{\rm d}b_{I}
and
(\mathsf{A}^{-1})^{J}{}_{I}G_{{\text{\tiny R}}\,J}|_{\Sigma}=-{\rm d}b_{I}
, hence realising the appropriate gluing condition
G_{{\text{\tiny L}}\,I}|_{\Sigma}=(\mathsf{A}^{-1})^{J}{}_{I}G_{{\text{\tiny R}}\,J}|_{\Sigma}
.

B.2
C
transformations and interfaces

Consider now an integral
\mathsf{C}
transform of the Lagrangian. This is defined by

{\cal L}_{\mathsf{C}}(F,\phi)={\cal L}(F,\phi)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\int F^{I}\wedge F^{J}\,.

(B.3)

Note that the corresponding symplectic transformation does not act on the field strengths
F^{I}
, in the sense that
\tilde{F}^{I}=F^{I}
, while it does transform the duals field strengths:
\tilde{G}_{I}=G_{I}+\mathsf{C}_{IJ}F^{J}
.
Since we assume that our four-dimensional manifolds are spin, the last term in (
B.3
) is always an integral multiple of
2\pi
and hence, again, does not change the physical properties of the theory.

The corresponding interface
{\cal W}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)
acting as in (
3.7
) can be obtained first using
{\cal L}(F,\phi)
on both sides of
\Sigma
, and then rewriting
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi)
as follows

{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi)={\cal L}_{\mathsf{C}}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi)-\frac{1}{4\pi}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\int F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}\,.

(B.4)

The last term can then be rewritten as
-\frac{1}{4\pi}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}
. Adding a delta-function factor that imposes
F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
, we get the interface operator (
4.1b
), namely

{\cal W}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma)=\exp\left(-\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\int{\cal D}b\,\exp\left[\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\oint b_{I}\wedge(F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}-F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}})\right]\,.

(B.5)

As a further check, extremizing with respect
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}
produces the localised contributions
G_{{\text{\tiny L}}\,I}|_{\Sigma}=-{\rm d}b_{I}
and
G_{{\text{\tiny R}}\,J}|_{\Sigma}+\mathsf{C}_{IJ}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}=-{\rm d}b_{I}
, hence realizing
G_{{\text{\tiny L}}\,I}|_{\Sigma}=G_{{\text{\tiny R}}\,I}|_{\Sigma}+\mathsf{C}_{IJ}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
.

B.3
\Omega
transformations and interfaces

An
\Omega
transformation corresponds to a simultaneous S-duality on all U(1) gauge fields. Following
[
111
,
113
]
, we now explain why it produces a physically equivalent theory and construct the corresponding interface.

As in
[
111
]
, we can extend our elementary degrees of freedom without modifying the theory as follows. Introduce two new types of fields: a set of U(1) two-form potentials
B^{I}
and and a new set
of U(1) one-form potentials
\tilde{A}^{I}
, with field strengths
\tilde{F}^{I}={\rm d}\tilde{A}^{I}
. Furthermore we impose that the two-form potentials
B^{I}
gauge the
n
electric one-form symmetries of our generic GZ model. Namely, we impose invariance under the one-form gauge symmetry

{\cal B}^{I}\rightarrow{\cal B}^{I}+2\pi\Lambda^{I}\quad,\quad F^{I}\rightarrow F^{I}-2\pi\Lambda^{I}\,,

(B.6)

where
\Lambda^{I}
are arbitrary closed two-forms that identify integral cohomology classes,
\Lambda^{I}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Of course, we also impose invariance under the ordinary small and large gauge transformations of
A^{I}
and
\tilde{A}^{I}
. The GZ Lagrangian
{\cal L}(F,\phi)
is then replaced by

{\cal L}({\cal F},\phi)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{IJ}{\cal F}^{I}\wedge\tilde{F}^{J}\,,

(B.7)

with

{\cal F}^{I}\equiv F^{I}+B^{I}\,.

(B.8)

In order to show that this Lagrangian describes the same physics of the original one, let us first integrate out the gauge fields
\tilde{A}^{I}
. This generates a delta-function that imposes
{\rm d}B^{I}=0
and the cohomological condition
B^{I}\in 2\pi H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Given this restriction, one can gauge-fix (
B.6
) by setting
B^{I}\equiv 0
(or equivalently setting
F^{I}\equiv 0
and renaming
B^{I}\rightarrow F^{I}
), getting back the original GZ Lagrangian
{\cal L}(F,\phi)
. Note also that the equations of motion of
B^{I}
impose the relations

\tilde{F}^{I}=2\pi\delta^{IJ}\frac{\partial{\cal L}({\cal F},\phi,\partial\phi)}{\partial{\cal F}^{J}}\,.

(B.9)

In the gauge
B^{I}\equiv 0
, in which we get back the original theory, this reduces to
\tilde{F}^{I}=\delta^{IJ}G_{J}
, with
G_{I}
defined as in (
2.3
).

In order to get the dual description, we choose to fix the one-form gauge symmetry (
B.6
) by imposing
F^{I}\equiv 0
, so that
B^{I}={\cal F}^{I}
. The equations of motion of
B^{I}
then give the relation (
B.9
). One can in principle invert (
B.9
) to get
{\cal F}
in terms of
\tilde{F}^{I}
and
\phi^{i}
(and
\partial_{\mu}\phi^{i}
). By integrating out
B^{I}
one then arrives at the S-dual Lagrangian
22
22

22

This step is straightforwardly justified only if the integration is Gaussian, and hence the Lagrangian
{\cal L}(F,\phi)
is at most quadratic in
{\cal F}
. For more general theories it is still valid at the classical level, and we implicitly assume that its validity holds at the quantum level too, at least within the effective field theory description. Furthermore, a more careful dualization may involve anomalous
\phi
dependent factors as in
[
111
]
, originating from a mixed ’t Hooft anomaly with the gravitational sector.

{\cal L}_{\Omega}(\tilde{F},\phi)=\left[{\cal L}(F,\phi)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{IJ}F^{I}\wedge\tilde{F}^{J}\right]_{F^{I}={{\cal F}^{I}(\tilde{F})}}\,.

(B.10)

By performing a further S-duality, we get the identification
\tilde{G}_{I}=-F^{I}
, so that in (
B.10
) we can make the identification
{\cal F}^{I}(\tilde{F})=-\delta^{IJ}\tilde{G}_{J}(\tilde{F})
.

Now, the interface
{\cal W}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)
implementing an
\Omega
-transformation can be obtained by performing the above procedure only on one side of
\Sigma

[
113
]
. In other words, it can be obtained from a ‘half-space gauging’ of the electric one-form symmetries. On the right side of
\Sigma
, let us rename
A^{I}
as
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}
and use the original theory
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\phi)
, while on the left side we want to use (
B.7
), renaming
A^{I}
and
\tilde{A}^{I}
as
\hat{A}^{I}
and
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
, respectively. Furthermore, we also need to enable the gluing condition
{\cal F}^{I}|_{\Sigma}=F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
, which in the gauge
B^{I}\equiv 0
reduces to
\hat{F}^{I}|_{\Sigma}=F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
. As will become clear shortly, this requires the addition of a boundary term
\frac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}
, so that the total action on the left-hand side becomes

\int_{{\cal T}_{\text{\tiny L}}}{\cal L}({\cal F},\phi)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{IJ}\int_{{\cal T}_{\text{\tiny L}}}{\cal F}^{I}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\,,

(B.11)

with
{\cal F}^{I}\equiv\hat{F}^{I}+{\cal B}^{I}
.
If we try to rerun the above argument and integrate out
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
, we get a delta-function that localizes the path-integral on
B^{I}
that satisfy
{\rm d}B^{I}=0
,
B^{I}\in 2\pi H^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})
and
{\cal F}^{I}|_{\Sigma}=F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}
.
Imposing the gauge-fixing
B^{I}\equiv 0
we indeed go back to the original description on the entire spacetime.
Instead, fixing the gauge
\hat{F}^{I}\equiv 0
and integrating out
B^{I}
on the left-hand side, we obtain the dual Lagrangian plus a boundary term

\int_{{\cal T}_{\text{\tiny R}}}{\cal L}_{\Omega}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\,.

(B.12)

The boundary term gives the
\Omega
interface already written in (
4.1c
):

{\cal W}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)=\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2\pi}\delta_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)\,,

(B.13)

which realizes (
3.7
) with
{\cal S}=\Omega
. This can be more directly checked, since in presence of
{\cal W}_{\Omega}(\Sigma)
the bulk
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}
equations of motion get the following boundary contribution:

F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}=\delta^{IJ}G_{{\text{\tiny R}}\,J}|_{\Sigma}\quad,\quad G_{{\text{\tiny L}}\,I}|_{\Sigma}=-\delta_{IJ}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}|_{\Sigma}\,.

(B.14)

These are indeed the correct gluing conditions.

Appendix C
Integral coprime matrix factorizations

In this appendix we collect some definitions and properties regarding the factorization of rational matrices in terms of integral matrices. More details can be for instance found in
[
119
]
, which works with matrices taking values in more general integral domains and the corresponding field of fractions, while here we focus on
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Q}
.

Any
n\times n
rational matrix
\mathsf{Q}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Q})
can be factorized in terms of pairs
(\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N})
and
(\tilde{\mathsf{M}},\tilde{\mathsf{N}})
of
integral
matrices, with invertible
\mathsf{N}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{N}}
, as follows:

\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{M}\mathsf{N}^{-1}=\tilde{\mathsf{N}}^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{M}}\,.

(C.1)

The factorization defined by
(\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N})
is
right-coprime

[
119
, Definition 4.6]
if there exist two integral matrices
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Z})
such that:

\mathsf{X}\mathsf{M}-\mathsf{Y}\mathsf{N}=\mathds{1}\,,

(C.2)

while the factorization defined by
(\tilde{\mathsf{M}},\tilde{\mathsf{N}})
is
left-coprime
if there exist two integral matrices
\tilde{\mathsf{X}},\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Z})
such that:

\tilde{\mathsf{M}}\tilde{\mathsf{X}}-\tilde{\mathsf{N}}\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}=\mathds{1}\,.

(C.3)

Since
\mathbb{Z}
is also a Bézout domain,
by Theorem 4.15 of
[
119
]
any rational matrix
\mathsf{P}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Q})
admits both a right-coprime and left-coprime factorization.

A right-coprime factorization
\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{M}\mathsf{N}^{-1}
is also
weakly
right-coprime, that is, such that

{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{M}{\bm{\alpha}},\mathsf{N}{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\,.

(C.4)

Similarly, a left-coprime factorization
\mathsf{P}=\tilde{\mathsf{N}}^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{M}}
is also weakly left-coprime, namely:

{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\rm t}{\bm{\alpha}},\tilde{\mathsf{N}}^{\rm t}{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\,.

(C.5)

Coprime factoriations are, in a certain sense, the ‘minimal’ possible factorizations. In order to explain this point, we can focus on right-coprime factorizations, as the argument for left-coprime ones is completely analogous.
Suppose that
\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{M}\mathsf{N}^{-1}
is a right-coprime factorization and take another (not necessarily right-coprime) factorization
\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{M}^{\prime}\mathsf{N}^{\prime-1}
. Then

\mathsf{M}^{\prime}=\mathsf{M}\mathsf{T}\quad,\quad\mathsf{N}^{\prime}=\mathsf{N}\mathsf{T}\,,

(C.6)

with
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{N}^{-1}\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
,
\det\mathsf{T}\neq 0
. Take now any integral vector
{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Since
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
are integral matrices,
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}{\bm{m}}=\mathsf{M}\mathsf{T}{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}{\bm{m}}=\mathsf{N}\mathsf{T}{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. By (
C.4
) we then deduce that
\mathsf{T}{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Since
{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is arbitrary, we conclude that

\mathsf{T}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Z})\,.

(C.7)

If
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
are right-coprime too, then we can apply the same argument to argue that
\mathsf{T}^{-1}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Z})
, and hence
|\det\mathsf{T}|=1
, that is

\mathsf{Q}=\mathsf{M}^{\prime}\mathsf{N}^{\prime-1}\quad\text{right-coprime}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathsf{T}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})\,.

(C.8)

Similar conclusions hold for left-coprime factorizations
\mathsf{Q}=\tilde{\mathsf{N}}^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{M}}=\tilde{\mathsf{N}}^{\prime-1}\tilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
. If
\mathsf{Q}=\tilde{\mathsf{N}}^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{M}}
is left-coprime, then
\tilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}=\mathsf{T}\tilde{\mathsf{M}}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{N}}^{\prime}=\mathsf{T}\tilde{\mathsf{N}}
with
\mathsf{T}\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{Z})
. Furthermore,
\mathsf{Q}=\tilde{\mathsf{N}}^{\prime-1}\tilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
is left-coprime too if (and only if)
\mathsf{T}\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
.

These results imply that right- or left-coprime factorizations are unique up to unimodular transformations. Notice that, in order to show this property, we have only used the weak coprimitivity conditions (
C.4
) and (
C.5
). Since
\mathbb{Z}
is a coherent Sylvester domain, by Corollary 3.22 of
[
119
]
, any rational matrix admits both a weakly right-coprime and a weakly left-coprime factorization. Hence, by uniqueness modulo
{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
tranformations, one could alternatively use (
C.4
) and (
C.5
) to characterize coprime factorizations of rational matrices.

Appendix D
A
interfaces from half-space gauging

In this appendix we discuss how the
\mathsf{A}
interfaces introduced in Section
4.2
can be obtained from a half-space gauging procedure, along the lines of
[
115
]
. In general, any
\mathsf{A}
matrix admits an integral factorization as in (
4.2
). We will separately consider the cases
\mathsf{E}=\mathds{1}
and
\mathsf{M}=\mathds{1}
in (
4.2
), which will correspond to magnetic and electric half-space gaugings, respectively. From (
4.7
), it follows that a more general
\mathsf{A}
interface can be obtained by an ordered sequence of half-space gaugings. We emphasize that, in general, these half-space gaugings cannot be performed simultaneously, because of the mixed anomaly between electric and magnetic one-form symmetries
[
113
]
.

D.1
Interfaces from magnetic one-form half-space gauging

We first assume that
\mathsf{E}=\mathds{1}
, i.e.
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{M}
. In this case the in
\mathsf{A}
interface can be obtained by following the half-space gauging prescription outlined in
[
115
]
.

We first observe that new theory is obtained from the gauging of the finite subgroup of the magnetic U(1)
n
one-form symmetry group of the theory. The new Lagrangian can be written as

{\cal L}(F,\phi)+\frac{1}{2\pi}F^{I}\wedge B_{I}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{M}^{I}{}_{J}\tilde{F}^{J}\wedge B_{I}\,,

(D.1)

where we have introduced the U(1) two-form potentials
B_{I}
and field strengths
\tilde{F}^{I}={\rm d}\tilde{A}^{I}
.
The last term imposes
\mathsf{M}^{J}{}_{I}{\rm d}B_{J}=0
and the cohomological condition
\mathsf{M}^{J}{}_{I}B_{J}\in 2\pi H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. This means that, adopting the notation introduced in Section
4
, the two-form potentials
B_{I}
identify an element of
H^{2}(X,\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{M}})
. Hence (
D.1
) implements a gauging of the finite
\Gamma^{*(1)}_{\mathsf{M}}
one-form symmetry group. By integrating out
B_{I}
we get the condition
F^{I}=\mathsf{M}^{I}{}_{J}\tilde{F}^{J}
, and the Lagrangian reduces to

\tilde{\cal L}(\tilde{F},\phi)={\cal L}(\mathsf{M}\tilde{F},\phi)
,
which coincides with
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}}(\tilde{F},\phi)
, since
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{M}
and hence
\mathsf{A}^{-1}=\mathsf{M}^{-1}
– see (
2.25
).

Let us now introduce an oriented three-dimensional suface
\Sigma
and assume that it divides the spacetime
X
in two parts,
X_{\text{\tiny L}}
ad
X_{\text{\tiny R}}
. We would like to repeat the above gauging only on
X_{\text{\tiny R}}
. First of all, let us rename
A^{I}\rightarrow\hat{A}^{I}
and
\tilde{A}^{I}\rightarrow A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}
on
X_{\text{\tiny R}}
, and
A^{I}\rightarrow A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
on
X_{\text{\tiny L}}
. We also impose the boundary conditions

B_{I}|_{\Sigma}=0,\qquad\hat{A}^{I}|_{\Sigma}=A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}\,,

(D.2)

enforcing the second one by adding an appropriate boundary term. The resulting action is

\int_{X_{\text{\tiny L}}}{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge\left(F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}-\hat{F}^{I}\right)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{X_{\text{\tiny L}}}B_{I}\wedge\left(\hat{F}^{I}-\mathsf{M}^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)+\int_{X_{\text{\tiny R}}}{\cal L}(\hat{F},\phi)\,.

(D.3)

This action describes a half-space gauging of the finite
\Gamma^{*(1)}_{\mathsf{M}}
one-form symmetry group. By integrating out
B_{I}
as above, we then get

\int_{X_{\text{\tiny L}}}{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge\left(F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}-\mathsf{M}^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}\right)+\int_{X_{\text{\tiny R}}}{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\phi)\,,

(D.4)

with
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{M}
. We have then obtained an
\mathsf{A}
interface (
4.3
) that separates
{\cal L}
on the left from
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}}
on the right. Had we started with
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}}
instead of
{\cal L}
, one would have obtained the same interface, but now separating
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}}
on the left from
{\cal L}
on the right.

D.2
A
interfaces from electric one-form half-space gauging

Let us consider now the case
\mathsf{M}=\mathds{1}
in (
4.2
), so that
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{E}^{-1}
. In this case one must follow a modification of the half-space gauging procedure outlined in
[
115
]
. We start from the Lagrangian

{\cal L}({\cal F},\phi)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}F^{\prime}_{I}\wedge{\cal F}^{J}\,,\quad\text{with}\quad{\cal F}^{I}\equiv F^{I}+B^{I}\,,

(D.5)

where
B^{I}
are two-form gauge potentials and
F^{\prime}_{I}={\rm d}A^{\prime}_{I}
are U(1) field strengths. (
D.5
) may be considered as a variant of (
B.7
) and combines
the electric
U(1)
one-form gauging
F^{I}\rightarrow{\cal F}^{I}=F^{I}+B^{I}
, together with a BF-term which restricts this one-form gauging to a finite subgroup. Indeed, integrating out
A^{\prime}_{I}
one gets a delta-function that localizes the
B^{I}
path integral over the solutions of

\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}{\rm d}B^{J}=0\quad,\quad\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}[B^{J}]\in 2\pi H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\,.

(D.6)

In other words, using the notation of Section
4.2
, the two-form potentials
B^{I}
are flat and identify a class of
H^{2}(X,\Gamma_{\mathsf{E}})
. Hence (
D.5
) implements the gauging of the finite
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{E}}
subgroup of the
{\rm U}(1)^{n}
electric one-form symmetry group.
23
23

23

From this perspective, (
B.7
) implements a gauging under a trivial
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{E}}
one-form symmetry group.
We cannot impose the gauge-fixing condition
B^{I}\equiv 0
, as in Appendix
B.3
. However, we can still impose the gauge-fixing condition
F^{I}\equiv 0
and solve (
D.6
) by setting
B^{I}=(\mathsf{E}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}\tilde{F}^{J}
, for some U(1) gauge fields
\tilde{A}^{I}
. This leads to a new Lagrangian
\tilde{\cal L}
such that

\tilde{\cal L}(\tilde{F},\phi)={\cal L}(\mathsf{E}^{-1}\tilde{F},\phi)\,.

(D.7)

That is,
\tilde{\cal L}
is just
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}}
as defined in (
2.25
) with
\mathsf{A}^{-1}=\mathsf{E}
. This shows that
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}}
with
\mathsf{A}^{-1}=\mathsf{E}
can be obtained from an electric
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{E}}
one-form gauging.

As in Appendix
D.1
, we would now like to obtain the corresponding
{\cal W}_{\mathsf{A}}(\Sigma)
by performing a half-space gauging, on right side of
\Sigma
, renaming
A^{I}\rightarrow\hat{A}^{I}
and
\tilde{A}^{I}\rightarrow A^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}}
on
X_{\text{\tiny R}}
, and
A^{I}\rightarrow A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}
on
X_{\text{\tiny L}}
. We implement the half-space gauging by using
{\cal L}(F_{\text{\tiny L}},\phi)
on
X_{\text{\tiny L}}
, and (
D.5
) with
{\cal F}^{I}=\hat{F}^{I}+B^{I}
on
X_{\text{\tiny R}}
. However, differently from what we did in Appendix
D.1
following
[
115
]
, here we do not impose the
B^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0
and
\hat{A}^{I}|_{\Sigma}=A^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}
separately. Rather, together with (
D.6
) the following boundary condition is satisfied

{\cal F}^{I}|_{\Sigma}=F^{I}_{\text{\tiny L}}|_{\Sigma}\,.

(D.8)

We realize this condition by introducing new boundary U(1) one-form potentials
b_{I}
and
c^{I}
and adding the boundary terms

\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}c^{I}\wedge\left({\rm d}b_{I}+F^{\prime}_{I}\right)\,.

(D.9)

Integrating out
A^{\prime}_{I}
one now gets, in addition to (
D.6
), the condition

{\rm d}c^{I}=\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}{\cal F}^{I}|_{\Sigma}\,.

(D.10)

Implementing these constraints, the second term in (
D.5
) and (
D.9
) together reduce to

\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge(F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}-{\cal F}^{J})\,.

(D.11)

This enforces the required boundary conditions.

Finally, as in absence of boundaries, we can fix the gauge
\hat{F}^{I}=0
and solve (
D.6
) by setting
B^{I}=(\mathsf{E}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny R}}
. On
X_{\text{\tiny R}}
we get, as above, the Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}}(F_{\text{\tiny R}},\phi)
with
\mathsf{A}^{-1}=\mathsf{E}
, while (
D.11
) becomes

\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\Sigma}b_{I}\wedge(\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}_{\text{\tiny L}}-F^{I}_{\text{\tiny R}})\,.

(D.12)

We have thus obtained our interface (
4.3
) with
\mathsf{M}=\mathds{1}
, i.e.
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{E}^{-1}
, separating
{\cal L}
on the left from
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}^{-1}}
on the right. Repeating the argument by starting with
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}}
rather than
{\cal L}
, one obtains the same interface, but now separating
{\cal L}_{\mathsf{A}}
on the left and
{\cal L}
on the right.

Finally we observe that, since
{{\cal W}}^{(\mathsf{E},\mathbbm{1})}={\overline{{\cal W}}}^{(\mathbbm{1},\mathsf{E})}
, we can alternatively obtain it by performing on
left
of
\Sigma
a
magnetic
half-space gauging of the same kind described in Appendix
D.1
, using
\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}
instead of
\mathsf{M}^{I}{}_{J}
. Similarly,
{{\cal W}}^{(\mathbbm{1},\mathsf{M})}={\overline{{\cal W}}}^{(\mathsf{M},\mathbbm{1})}

can also be obtained by performing on left of
\Sigma
an electric half-space gauging of the same kind described in this appendix, using
\mathsf{M}^{I}{}_{J}
instead of
\mathsf{E}^{I}{}_{J}
.

Appendix E
Bulk origin of minimal TQFTs and
C
interfaces

In this appendix we show how the
\mathsf{C}
interfaces of Section
4.3
can be obtained from a half-space gauging procedure.
In order to do that, we will first provide a bulk formulation of the minimal
{\cal A}_{\mathsf{C}}
and non-minimal
{\cal Z}_{\mathsf{P},\mathsf{N}}
TQFTs supported by these interfaces.

E.1
Bulk realization of the minimal TQFT

In Section
4.3.1
we have discussed how one can associate a minimal TQFT, denoted as
{\cal A}_{\Sigma}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}
, to any choice of
\mathsf{C}_{IJ}
mod
\mathbb{Z}
. In this appendix we discuss how
{\cal A}_{\Sigma}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}
can be realized on the boundary of a four-dimensional topological theory. In the case
n=1
, this theory has already been discussed in
[
121
]
, Appendix B of
[
1
]
and Appendix E of
[
120
]
.

Let us pick a right-coprime integral factorization (
4.25
) and a four-dimensional space
Y
such that
\Sigma=\partial Y
. Consider the continuum description of (
4.32
). We promote
{\cal B}^{I}
to dynamical two-form potentials
B^{I}
obeying Dirichlet boundary conditions
B^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0
. One gets the action

-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}B^{I}\wedge B^{J}+\frac{1}{2\pi}N^{I}{}_{J}\int_{Y}B^{J}\wedge\tilde{F}_{I}\,,

(E.1)

where
\tilde{F}_{I}={\rm d}\tilde{A}_{I}
