are U(1) two-form field strengths.
This bulk action is invariant mod
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
under the one-form and zero-form gauge transformations

B^{I}\rightarrow B^{I}+{\rm d}\Lambda^{I}\quad,\quad\tilde{A}_{I}\rightarrow\tilde{A}_{I}+\mathsf{P}_{IJ}\Lambda^{I}+{\rm d}\lambda_{I}\quad,\quad\Lambda^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0\,.

(E.2)

The second term in (
E.1
) constrains
B^{I}
to be
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
two-form potentials:

{\rm d}B^{I}=0\quad,\quad N^{I}{}_{J}[B^{J}]\in 2\pi H^{2}(Y,\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})\,.

(E.3)

Hence the potentials
B^{J}
determine a class in
H^{2}(Y,\Sigma,\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}})
. The
B_{I}
equations of motion are

\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{I}\left(\mathsf{P}_{JK}B^{K}-\tilde{F}_{J}\right)=0\,.

(E.4)

Notice that the boundary condition
B^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0
may be regarded as boundary contribution to the
\tilde{A}_{I}
equations of motion, and implies that
N^{J}{}_{I}\tilde{F}_{J}|_{\Sigma}=0
.

One can construct the following gauge invariant operators

\displaystyle U_{\bf m}(C,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\exp\left({\rm i}m_{I}\int_{C}B^{I}\right)\quad,\quad\partial C=\gamma\subset\Sigma=\partial Y\,,

(E.5a)

\displaystyle V_{\bf n}(C,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\exp\left({\rm i}n^{I}\int_{\gamma}\tilde{A}_{I}-{\rm i}n^{I}\mathsf{P}_{IJ}\int_{C}B^{J}\right)\quad,\quad\partial C=\gamma\subset Y\,,

(E.5b)

\displaystyle L_{\bf n}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\exp\left({\rm i}n^{I}\oint_{\gamma}\tilde{A}_{I}\right)\quad,\quad~~~~\gamma\subset\Sigma=\partial Y\,.

(E.5c)

Note that the
L_{\bf n}(\gamma)
is invariant under the gauge transformations (
E.2
) since
\Lambda^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0
.

Let us first assume that
\Sigma=\partial Y=\emptyset
, so that the lines
L_{\bf n}
disappear from the spectrum. First observe, the operators (
E.5b
) that depend on the open surface must be considered as trivial, since they can only contribute to correlators through contact terms. Hence the operators (
E.5b
) can be non-trivial only if they do not depend on the surface
C
, and hence can be considered as genuine line operators. This happens only if
n^{I}\mathsf{P}_{IJ}\int_{C}B^{J}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
for any
C
and
B^{I}
. Since
\frac{1}{2\pi}N^{I}{}_{J}B^{J}
defines an integral two-cochain and
(\mathsf{P}\mathsf{N}^{-1})_{IJ}=(\mathsf{P}\mathsf{N}^{-1})_{JI}
, this happens precisely if
(\mathsf{P}\mathsf{N}^{-1})_{IJ}n^{J}=0
. Setting
\alpha^{I}\equiv(\mathsf{N}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}n^{J}
, we have
\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\alpha^{J}=n^{I}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}\alpha^{J}\in\mathbb{Z}
. But since
\mathsf{N}
and
\mathsf{P}
are right-coprime, we must have
\alpha^{I}\equiv m^{I}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n^{I}=\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}m^{J}
. It follows that only the operators
V_{\mathsf{N}{\bf m}}
can be genuine line operators. But because of the equations of motion (
E.4
),
V_{\mathsf{N}{\bf m}}=1
and hence all the
V_{\bf n}
operators are trivial.

Consider now the operators (
E.5a
). Since
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{N}
are right-coprime,
\mathsf{X}\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{Y}\mathsf{N}=\mathds{1}
for some integral matrices
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
. Using (
E.3
), this implies that

U_{\bf m}(C)=\exp\left({\rm i}m_{I}\mathsf{X}^{IJ}\mathsf{P}_{JK}\int_{C}B^{K}\right)\,.

(E.6)

But this in turn implies that
U_{\bf m}(C)
can be split in the product of open surface operators
V_{\bf n}
, with
n^{I}=m_{J}\mathsf{X}^{JI}
. Since
V_{\bf n}=1
for all
n^{I}
, we see that the operators
U_{\bf m}
are trivial too. We therefore conclude that if
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{N}
are right coprime, and
\partial X=\emptyset
, the topological theory (
E.1
) is trivial.

Now let us go back to the four-dimensional space with boundary
\partial X=\Sigma
. In addition to the new boundary line operators (
E.5c
), we can also consider
U_{\bf m}(C,\gamma)
with
\partial C=\gamma\subset\Sigma
. But the above arguments on the triviality of the bulk theory imply that these are genuine boundary line operators too. So, we can simply denote them as
U_{\bf m}(\gamma)
. In fact, from
V_{\bf n}(C,\gamma)=1
it follows that, if we pick
m_{I}=n^{J}\mathsf{P}_{JI}
,
U_{{\bf m}}
can be identified with
L_{\bf n}
:

L_{\bf n}(\gamma)=U_{\mathsf{P}^{\rm t}{\bf n}}(\gamma)\,.

(E.7)

Observe also that (
E.3
) implies that
U_{\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}{\bf k}}=1
, for any
{\bf k}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Together with (
E.7
), this also implies that
U_{\mathsf{N}{\bf k}}=1
for any
\tilde{\bf k}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}
, since
\mathsf{P}^{\rm t}\mathsf{N}\tilde{\bf k}=\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P}\tilde{\bf k}
. Hence the

U_{\bf m}
and
L_{\bf n}
are labeled by the elements of
\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{N}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}}
, respectively. Furthermore, using again the right-coprimitivity of
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{N}
, we can also invert (
E.7
) and express the
U_{\bf m}
lines in terms of the
L_{\bf n}
lines. Indeed,
any
{\bf m}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{Z}}
can be rewritten as
\mathsf{P}^{\rm t}\mathsf{X}^{\rm t}{\bf m}-\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{Y}^{\rm t}{\bf m}
for some integral matrices
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
, so that
U_{\bf m}=U_{\mathsf{P}^{\rm t}\mathsf{X}^{\rm t}{\bf m}}
. From (
E.7
) it then follows that

U_{\bf m}(\gamma)=L_{\mathsf{X}^{\rm t}{\bf m}}\,.

(E.8)

Hence, we can either use
L_{\bf n}
or
U_{\bf m}
as generators of all the boundary lines.

Finally, as in
[
121
,
1
]
, it may be convenient to relax the condition
B^{I}|_{\Sigma}
and restore the one-form gauge symmetry on the boundary by adding boundary Stueckelberg fields
a^{I}
, such that

a^{I}\rightarrow a^{I}+\Lambda^{I}|_{\Sigma}\,,

(E.9)

under (
E.2
).
We can then make the full system gauge invariant by adding the boundary action

\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\oint_{\Sigma}a^{I}\wedge f^{J}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\oint_{\Sigma}a^{J}\wedge\tilde{F}_{I}\,.

(E.10)

The boundary lines can now be written in a fully gauge invariant way

L_{\bf n}(\gamma)=\exp\left[{\rm i}n^{I}\oint_{\gamma}\left(\tilde{A}_{I}-\mathsf{P}_{IJ}a^{J}\right)\right]\quad,\quad U_{\bf m}(\gamma)=\exp\left[{\rm i}m_{I}\left(\int_{C}B^{I}-\oint_{\gamma}a^{I}\right)\right]\,.

(E.11)

This description makes it easy to compute the brading of the these lines. The insertion of an
L_{\bf n}(\gamma)
modifies the
a^{I}
equations of motion into

\mathsf{N}^{J}{}_{I}\left(\tilde{F}_{J}|_{\Sigma}-\mathsf{P}_{JK}f^{K}\right)=-n^{J}\mathsf{P}_{JI}\delta_{2}(\gamma)\,.

(E.12)

By using this equation and
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{P}\mathsf{N}^{-1}
, one can check that the braiding rule of the
L_{\bf n}
lines is given by (
4.28
).
This shows that the lines
L_{\bf n}
– or, equivalently, the lines
U_{\bf m}
– provide a realization of
{\cal A}_{\Sigma}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}
. This of course holds also for the partially gauge-fixed description with
B^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0
.

E.2
Coupling to background gauge fields

We now consider the coupling of the this minimal TQFT to background
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
gauge fields defined on
\Sigma
. If we use the description (
E.5c
) of the topological lines, this corresponds to adding to (
E.1
) the boundary term

\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{A}_{I}\wedge{\cal B}^{J}\,,

(E.13)

where
{\cal B}^{I}
denote the
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
background gauge fields, i.e. such that
\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}{\cal B}^{J}
identify an integral uplift of an element of
H^{2}(\Sigma,\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}})
.
Hence we get

-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}B^{I}\wedge B^{J}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\int_{Y}B^{J}\wedge\tilde{F}_{I}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\int_{\Sigma}\tilde{A}_{I}\wedge{\cal B}^{J}\,,

(E.14)

whose partition function gives
{\cal A}_{\Sigma}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}[{\cal B}]
.
Integrating out
\tilde{A}_{I}
localizes the
B^{I}
integration to a discrete sum over (flat)
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
bulk gauge fields
B^{I}
, that is such that
\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}B^{J}\in H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
, with
B^{I}|_{\Sigma}=-{\cal B}^{I}
. We can then write
B^{I}
as
B^{I}=\Omega^{I}-\hat{\cal B}^{I}
, where
\hat{\cal B}^{I}
are
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
bulk gauge fields that provide any given bulk extension of the background
{\cal B}^{I}
, and
\Omega^{I}
are dynamical
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
bulk gauge fields with

\Omega^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0
. In other words,
\omega^{I}\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\Omega^{J}
represent the integral uplift of the relative cohomology classes
\bm{\omega}\in H^{2}(Y,\Sigma;\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}})
, which hence identify the physically inequivalent choices of
\Omega^{I}
. The action (
E.14
) reduces to

-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}(\Omega^{I}-\hat{\cal B}^{I})\wedge(\Omega^{J}-\hat{\cal B}^{J})\,.

(E.15)

The associated partition function obtained by summing over
\bm{\omega}\in H^{2}(Y,\Sigma;\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}})
defines the minimal TQFT coupled to
{\cal B}^{I}
,
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}[{\cal B}]
. In this form the anomaly (
4.33
) is manifest.

One can also define the coupling to background fields through the line operators (
E.5a
). Since they are defined in terms of periods of
B^{I}
over relative bulk cycles
C
, that is, such that
\partial C\subset\Sigma
, a natural coupling to background potentials is given by a bulk term

\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}\int_{Y}B^{J}\wedge\tilde{\cal B}_{I}\,,

(E.16)

where we are already restricting ourselves to flat
B^{I}
potentials, such that
B^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0
and
\mathsf{N}^{I}{}_{J}B^{J}\in 2\pi H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
. Hence, they identify elements of
H^{2}(Y,\Sigma;\Gamma_{\mathsf{N}})
and, adopting the notation introduced after (
E.14
), we can rename them as
B^{I}=\Omega^{I}
.

On the other hand, in (
E.16
)
\tilde{\cal B}_{I}
are flat
\Gamma^{*(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
two-form potentials, whose boundary restriction
\tilde{\cal B}_{I}|_{\Sigma}
defines an alternative choice of three-dimensional background potentials for the
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
one-form symmetry.
In Appendix
E.1
we discussed the equivalence between the
L_{\bf n}
and
U_{\bf m}
line operators. In the present context, the same arguments show that the two choices of
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
background gauge fields provides by
{\cal B}^{I}
and
\tilde{\cal B}_{I}|_{\Sigma}
are related by

\tilde{\cal B}_{I}|_{\Sigma}=\mathsf{P}_{IJ}{\cal B}^{J}\quad\text{mod}\quad 2\pi H^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})\,,

(E.17)

which corresponds to a proper identity in
H^{2}(\Sigma,\Gamma^{*}_{\mathsf{N}})
.
24
24

24

In this sense, by using the Bézout identity
\mathsf{X}\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{Y}\mathsf{N}=\mathds{1}
, (
E.17
) is invertible into the identity
{\cal B}^{I}=\mathsf{X}^{IJ}\tilde{\cal B}_{J}|_{\Sigma}
modulo
2\pi H^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})
.
The relation (
E.17
) can be extended to the identity
\tilde{\cal B}_{I}=\mathsf{P}_{JI}\hat{\cal B}^{J}
mod
2\pi H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
, involving the corresponding bulk extensions. By using it in (
E.16
), we get the bulk theory

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}\Omega^{I}\wedge\Omega^{J}+\frac{1}{2\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}\Omega^{I}\wedge\hat{\cal B}^{J}

(E.18)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}\hat{\cal B}^{I}\wedge\hat{\cal B}^{J}-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}(\Omega^{I}-\hat{\cal B}^{I})\wedge(\Omega^{J}-\hat{\cal B}^{J})\,.

Comparing (
E.18
) to (
E.15
), we conclude that the corresponding path integral is given by

\quad{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}[{\cal B}]\,\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}\hat{\cal B}^{I}\wedge\hat{\cal B}^{J}\right)\,.

(E.19)

The bulk term precisely cancels the anomaly of
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}[{\cal B}]
, and hence the full system is anomaly free.

E.3
C
interfaces from half-space gauging

Let us now consider a GZ Lagrangian
{\cal L}(F,\phi)
defined on a spacetime
X
, and an orientable submanifold
\Sigma
. Furthermore we assume that
\Sigma
splits
X
into two halves
X_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
X_{\text{\tiny R}}
, with
\partial X_{\text{\tiny L}}=\Sigma=-\partial X_{\text{\tiny R}}
. Let us now couple this theory a topological sector defined by (
E.18
), with
Y=X_{\text{\tiny L}}
and
\hat{\cal B}^{I}=(\mathsf{N}^{-1})^{I}{}_{J}F^{J}
. That is, we add to
\int_{X_{\text{\tiny L}}}{\cal L}(F,\phi)
the terms

-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\mathsf{N}^{\rm t}\mathsf{P})_{IJ}\int_{Y}\Omega^{I}\wedge\Omega^{J}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{P}_{IJ}\int_{Y}F^{I}\wedge\Omega^{J}\,,

(E.20)

with
\Omega^{I}
defines as in Appendix
E.2
.
This procedure be regarded as a half-space gauging with discrete torsion, restricted to
X_{\text{\tiny L}}
, of the magnetic
\Gamma^{(1)}_{\mathsf{N}}
one-form symmetry group of the GZ models. The gauging is defined by the ‘charges’
\mathsf{P}_{IJ}
and the discrete two-form potentials satisfy the boundary conditions
\Omega^{I}|_{\Sigma}=0
. Applying (
E.19
) we can immediately conclude that this half-space gauging has the effect of inserting the terms

{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}[\mathsf{N}^{-1}F]\exp\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{4\pi}\mathsf{C}_{IJ}\int_{X_{\text{\tiny L}}}F^{I}\wedge F^{J}\right)\,,

(E.21)

into the path integral.
In other words, the net effect is to perform an
{\cal S}_{\mathsf{C}}
transformation of the theory on
X_{\text{\tiny L}}
, and insert the TQFT
{\cal A}^{(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{P})}_{\Sigma}[\mathsf{N}^{-1}F]
. Therefore, using (
4.52
), we get precisely the minimal realization of the
\mathsf{C}
interface (
4.23
).
This generalizes the result of
[
66
]
. While the rationale is basically the same, our half-space gauging procedure differs by some interesting details from the procedure followed in
[
66
]
.
